OUNT Y BOROUGH OF WARRINGTON. V) ST. AUSTIN'd WARD BYE-ELECTION, 1903. LADIES AND GENTLEMEN, Owing to the well-deserved elevation of your highly esteemed representative, Mr. Thomas Burton, to the Aldermanic Bench, a vacancy has been caused in the Council for St. Austin's Ward. I have been asked by a large number of responsible ratepayers of every shade of political and religious opinion, and also by the Liberal Ward Committee to allow myself to be nominated for the vacancy. I have great pleasure In yielding to their kind and flattering request on the understanding I am allowed to offer myself for your suffrage as an Independent Candi- date. It is well known that although I bold Liberal views on Imperial and Parliamentary politics, I am bound to no political party in my Religious Opinions, and that I am totally opposed to Municipal affairs being conducted on party lines, or engineered for political or party advantage ; therefore, although I hope to secure majority of the votes of the ratepayers of our Ward, irrespective of religious or political oninicn, : wish it to be distinctly understood I will in no way bind myself to support the views of any sect, section, or party, except In so far as the welfare and needs of the ratepayers are concerned and my conscienne dictates, and I appeal to my record as a Councillor for the Borough in past years to prove that in all discussions in the Council Chamber I have supported only those motions which I honestly believed were for the benefit and well-being of our town. Should you honour me with your representation I firmly intend to do in the future as I have in the past, that is, to give my beet efforts to the advancement of the welfare and prosperity of Warrington. I am deeply interested in the progress of Warrington as a nsanufacturfng centre, and so tar as I can, I will always advocate any scheme proposed In the interests of manufacturing enterprises that will tend to improve our borough, and find more permanent and better paid labsur for our working people. lam in favour of Municipal Employment being found for the genuine unemployed; and I hold that this can easily be done by municipal enterprise, with advantage to the ratepayers and benefit to the unemployed, by the judicious employment of labour on our waste and unoccu- pied land, to. The hard-working wage earners have always in me an earnest friend and advocate. No man has a greater interest in or a greater desire to holy the genuine working man than I have. I personally know his wants and his difficulties. I have the greatest respect and admiration for the man whole honestly trying to improve himself and his position. for by doing sobs improves the community in which he Urea. I have good reason for wishing to associate myself with St. Austin's Ward in our Town Council. I began my business career in this Ward. I am one of the largest amongst the Tradesmen Ratepayers in the Ward. and if elected I will do my best to alleviate the needs and burdens of that important and responsible class. If you do me the honour of electing me as one of your representatives, It will be my constant endeavour to keep the Borough Rates down to the lowest point, consistent with the good and efficient mamigernent of the town and its various enterprises. I take a deep interest in the Religious as well as the Technical Bducation of our children. I have always advocated free meals for school children when the parents were unable to sufficiently provide for them; for in the health, strength, and ability of the obilriren of to-day is vested the real future and prosperity of Our Borough and Empire. I am, Ladies and Gentlemen, Yours ever faithfully. JOHN MAGINN.

Raddon Court. Warringt )n.

The Examinee, Saturday January 18, 1908.

CHILDREN. IN PUBLIC HOUSES. Well-known_Warrington Publican

Summoned

MAGISTRATE AND CHIEF CONSTABLE.

At the Warrington Borough Police Court on Monday before the Mayor and other magistrates, Fairfield Thompson Turner. licensee of the Coach and Horses inn, Liverpool-road, was charged with selling intoxicating liquor, to wit, whisky, to a person under the age of fourteen other than in a sealed bottle. .

Mr. Steel appeared for the defendant and pleaded not guilty. The Chief Constable said that at 8.20 a.m. on Satur- day, January 4th, P.C.'s Reddington and Woolacott saw a small boy come from the Coach and Horses, Liverpool- road, who bad in his possession a small vessel contain- ing threepennyworth of whisky. The °facers took the boy back to the house and the defendant's daughter admitted serving him. She told them that she had questioned the boy as to his age and he said he was fourteen. He (the Chief Constable) would have the boy's birth certificate produced which would show that tbeboy was only eleven years of age last November, and besides the boy was not even tall enough to show that he was fourteen. _ . _ . .

fie 5 -ding t-o n, in corroborating, said that when he and P.C. Woolacott took the boy back to the house the ; defendant was upstairs. They questioned the defend- ant's daughter, Elizabeth, who replied that the boy came in some weeks ago, and she asked him his age, and Ihe said he was fourteen, and she had served him several I times since then. Mr. Turner said he had never seen 1 the lad before. There were smaller boys than the one , they served who were over fourteen. I Mr. J. Todeschool attendance officer) then produced the boy's birth ,certificate, which showed that he was born on the 10th November, 1896. Mr. Steel : Were you present when the boy was born ? (Laughter.) How do you identify the boy ? Mr. Todd said he was not present when the boy was born, but he. (Mr. Todd) had received notice of the boy's birth. The boy (John Hughes) was then called. A Mrs. Griffiths sent him for three pennyworth of whisky. He had been to the house for her twice before, and had told Miss Turner that he was fourteen. Mrs. Griffiths and she gave him a half-penny each time he went. Mr. T. S. Steel in his defence drew the magistrates attention to the fact that Mr. Turner had been landlord of the Conch and horses for thirteen years that day. He was a member of the Licensed Victuallers' Associa- tion by whom he (Mr. Steel) was instructed to defend. The Act of Parliament under which defendant was summoned was Intoxicating Liquors, (Sale to Children Act), 1901, which said that no person had to sell intoxicating liquors of any description to any person under the age of fourteen years otherwise than in sealed vessels. The Licensed 'Victuallers Association

PARR'S BANK LIMITED. Report of the Directors.

The directors of this bank will submit the follow- ing report at the annntl ordinary general meeting of the company to be held at the Canon-street, Hotel, London on Thursday, the 23rd inst. :—The directors, in submitting to the shareholders the balance sheet of the bank at 31st December, 1907, certified by the auditors, have to report that the position and prospects of the business continue to be in every way satisfactory. Including £H8,163 10s. 9d. brought forward at 30th June, 1907, the net balance of profits for the half year, after writing down investments to below market value, providing for all bad and doubtful debts, and rebating the current bills at 7 per cent., is £367,613 18s. From this sum the directors recommend :—The payment of a half-year's dividend at the rate of 19 per cent, per annum, payable, free of income tax, on 3rd February, £162,307 10s. ; a bonus of 1 per cent, payable, free of income tax on 3rd February, £17,085 ; the I transfer to bank premises account, £15,009; the transfer ' I to officers' pension and provident fund, £15,000 ; making £209,392 10s. ; leaving balance to be carried to next account of £158,221 Bs. Branches of the bank will shortly •be opened at 300. King's-road, Chelsea, S.W., and at Rusholme, Manchester. The directors who retire at this time by rotation are Mr. James Tullocb, Mr. Alfred Morrison Turner, the Hon. Arthur Stanley, MT., Mr. Hussey Packe, Mr. Alfred Hewlett, and Sir Ewen Cameron, K.C.M.G., all of whom are eligible for re-elec- tion. The auditors, Messrs. Turquand, Youngs and Co., ;London ; Messrs. Stead,Taylor and Stead, Liverpool ; and Messrs. Alfred Tongue and Co„ Manchester, retire, I and offer themselves for re-election.—Cecil F. Parr. chairman. 4, Bartholomew-lane, London, E.C., 14th January, 1908.

===== 0002601_19080118_art0012.txt | included=true | included =====
- -.--.>------

Wycliffe P.S.A. I Hr. Joseph Owen presided at a meeting of this I Society on Sunday. In the absence of the appointed I speaker through illness, Mr. George Wallington gave the address, upon " As water to a thirsty soul, so is good news from afar." He said that as they thirsted for water, in like manner they expected news from their loved ones and friends from afar. He hoped the varied joys and customs of Christmas would be kept up, even the joys of children in the shape of "Santa Claus." Christmas time ge them great joy in thinking for others, and helping and comforting the needy. Mr. Woods sang " Across the Minster " and " Our Father " in a plea sing manner. Mr. Gleave led the prayer; Mr. J. Garner the lesson. Mr. W. H. Payton presided at the organ. The Secretary announced that the Visitation Secretary was ill, but hoped he would soon be—restored. A vote of sympathy was passed with Mr. and Mrs. A. Houghton in the loss of their child.

BRONCHIAL CATARRH.

A Lady's great Cure by VENO'S LIGHTNING COUGH CURE. Mrs. J. PALACE AND HIPPODROME.

"The Dandy Thieves."

Merriment holds sway at the Palace and Hippodrome this week and those who want to be thoroughly amused could not do better than visit this popular house. The name ri Fred Karno is synonymous with fun of the first water, and the second half of the programme is sustained by his capital company in the successful musical extravaganza entitled "The Dandy Thieves." There is not a dull moment from beginning to end and shrieks of laughter greet the adventures of the bold bad burglars and the valiant police who deem it best to hide round the nearest corner when there is a prospect of coming to close quarters with the wrong-doers. The piece is presented in three scenes and several catchy musical numbers prove highly popular, notably the famous "Six little burglars.' Mr. George Hester, as Sergeant Lightning is the life ! and soul of the performance and his doings whilst in charge of Muffin's Emporium are irresistibly comical. Mr. Frank Morton, as Lord Fasem, alias IFlash Jack, sings with fine spirit "I'm King of the Burglars," his robust voice being used to the best advantage. The programme includes t some other features of more than usual interest. The Davenports, lightning magicians and illusionists, give one of the best conjuring entertainmeats seen in Warrington for a long time. The audience is com pletely mystified by a series of wonderful tricks, for in respanse to a wave of the magic wand, live doves, yards of handkerchiefs and flags appear apparently from nowhere. Yeager and D Costa figure in smart !American Coonisms, their dancing being particularly ! clever. Harry Hillier is an adcomolished vocalist and instrumentalist and does some surprising things in the way of artistic paper-tearing. Some very pleasing songs, beautifnlly illustrated, are given bv Foe and Melville, and G. W. Foste !, comedian, sc res a dis- tinct success in his up ta-d its tame. R tyrnond's Bio-Tableaux, always interesting, has this week some side-splitting pictures depicting the wild career of the Reckless Horseman." Widclop and his orchestra do themselves great credit in the bright selections " Royal Standard" (Dareiriski) and " R ynance and Two Dances" (German).

TRADES AND LABOUR COUNCIL.

Municipal Coal.

The monthly meeting of the Trades an I I abour Council was held in the Independent Labour Party rooms on Tuesday evening under the presidency of Mr. W. H. Richardson. AN APPEAL TO THE KING.

A petition was read from Mr. W. 0. Benwick,London, addressed to His Majesty King Edward, stating that a national memorial to His Majesty's illustrious pre- decessor, the late Queen Victoria was now-in process of erection in front of Buckingham- Palace, and that many of His Majesty's subjects hoped they might have found employment in the cutting and working of the tnaterial of which the memorial is to 13 built, but to their great grief and disappointment they found that much of the work which might have hese done by them was being carried out in Italy. They prayed that His "Majesty might use his influence to the effect that British labour might be employed in the preparation and erection of a memorial to a great British sovereign. He asked the Council to sign the petition. On the proposition of Mr. FiALEY the petition was passed for signing.

===== 0002601_19080118_art0019.txt | included=true | included =====
A PROPOSED IVITHDRAWAG.

The SECRETA.RY produced a letter from the Amalga- mated Operative Bakers and Confectioners, signed by Mr. FL iiickinbottom, stating that their branch had decided to withdraw their membership from the Council and asking that Mr. Hickinbottom's name might be struck off the Executive Committee.

A deputation was appointed to wait upon the bakers with a view to inducing them to reconsider their decision.

MUNICIPAL COAL SUPPLY.

A letter was read from the Independent Labour Party endorsing Councillor Stott's motion at the Town Council, with reference to a municipal coal supply. Councillor STOTT moved "That this Council endorses the proposition for ft municipal coal supply, and pledges itself to do all in its power to bring pressure upon the Town Council to adopt such proposition." He recapitulated some of the points dealt with at the Town Council meeting and answered objections. Mr. MOORFIELD seconded and endorsed all Councillor Stott bad said. Mr. T. SMITH in supporting said the profits on the 0031 should go to help to reduce the rates. If the Corporation could get coal for Ids. a ton why not every- body else? The workingman had to pay 20s. and six shil' ings meant a lot to a man with a big family. He had great pleasure in supporting the resolution. (Applause.) Mr. COMM, also in supporting, said the discussion in the Council was a disgrace to that body. He endorsed all Councillor Stott bad done and said and he (Mr. Corrin) thought the motion should have been passed. (Hear, hear.) Mr. T. PLUMB also supported the resolution. He said that the profits on the coal should go towards reducing the rates. The Corporation partook of the profits which the workingman ought to have a share of. , It would be of great benefit to .the householders in this ' town. He thoght it took something like 4s. Gd. to ss. Gd. to get the coal to the pit mouth and then he workingman had to pay 20s. for a ton of coal. (Applause.) Councillor STorr intimated that the Birmingham Tr ides Council had passed a resolution similar to the one they were discussing. Mr. PLUMB thought that the resolution should go before each of their branches. Mr. LEECH said that no member of the Council ever delivered a more statesmanlike speech than Councillor Stott did at the last Council meeting. If he had his own way he would nationalise the whole of the land and then they would be sure to get a municipal coal supply. The resolution was carried unanimously. ANOTHER. RESOLUTION.

Mr. PODMORE said it was a very good thing to pass a resolution of that description and some steps ought to be taken to bring the resolution before all tbeir branches and then it wOuld cause enthusiasm. He would suggest that a circular be sent to the branches who would then be able to discuss it thoroughly. Some strong measure ought to be taken by that Council in the way of a public meeting or something of that description. He should like to ask Alderman Tinnion in that room or in any other room whether the price of coal was an abstract question. He hoped the branches would take that matter up earnestly and come to some decision and that the delegates would bring 1 the matter forward at the meetings of their branches. I (Mr. MOORFIELD, in supporting. Mr. Pochnore's motion was beginning to speak about the criticism of the Council in the " Essrniner " when The Cu tam ax told him he was wandering from the question. Mr. MOIRPIELD "I am."—Continuing, he said they wanted enthusiasm throwing into the Council, and he hoped the branches would support it. Mr. HALEY said he felt disgusted at the way Alder- man Tinnion treated Councillor Stott's motion in the Council Chamber. It was a very important matter to workingmen. To say that the price of coal was only an abstract question was a most disgraceful expression. (Applause.) The resolution was carried unanimously.

Warrngton's Fine Performance. The Warrington men had to journey to Barrow oa Saturday with a team weakened by the absence of Lees, Jenkins, and Taylor. The chosen thirteen however rose to the occasion and beat Barrow in decisive fashion. Fine, breezy weather prevailed and the ground was in fair order considering the frost. Thirty- thousand spectators watched a most interesting game. Teams: _

Barrow—H. Gifford, back; E. Brookbank, J. P. Burns, W. Barnes, and H. &amber, three-quarter backs • E. Fallowfteld and J. D. Wharton, half backs ;, Brough, C. Brown, A. Hannan, W. Hoggett, T. Hughes,. and J. Broekbank, forwards. Warrington —J. H. Tilley, back; J. Fish, D. Idler, wood, G. Dickenson and E. Jordan, three-quarter backs H. Brooks and J. Lloyd, half backs ; A. Board- man, G. Thomas, G. Heath, F. Shugars, Harmer, and P. Boardman, forwards..— . . . . .

From the first scrum Lloyd passed to Jordan near touch and the wing man kicked across. Isherwood fielded cleanly and gave Fish a walk in at the corner. This roused the Barrowians and .1. Brockbank gathered from a melee near the line, and scored a try, which Gifford failed to improve. Isherwood next scored after a fine individual burst, Thomas kicking a goal, and then Jordan gathered near the home line, and added a third try, which Thomas converted. J. Brockbank again scored for Barrow, and the score at the interval was—Warrington 13 points, Bsrrow 6 points. The second half found Barrow at a disadvantage, (ughes retiring owing to injuries received in the rlier portion. Fish ran half the length of the field, ihd scored a fourth try, and again Thomas added the major points. Barrow played pluckily against heavier opponents, and Wharton registered a third unimproved try. Brooks added further to the Warrington total, and though Barrow pressed hard the game ended : Warrington 3 goals 5 tries (21 points), Barrow 3 tries '9 points).

WOMEN'S CHAT.

Veteran Artists.—Painters and artists are proverbi ally among the longest lived, and one of the most notable examples is Mr. W. P. Frith, R.A., who was received the other day by the King, and is still at work with his brush in his ninety-first year. His three best- known pictures are " Derby Day," "The Railway Station" and" Ramsgate Sand s." The last named was purchased by Queen Victoria, in 1853, and was at Osborne until two or three years ago, when the King had the painting pl aced in one of his private rooms at Windsor Castle. Sir Lawrence Alma Taderna, is a much younger man, but he is seventy-two, and although he has produced nearly 400 pictures. his paintings have still all the vitality and freshness of youth. Sir Edward Poynter, the President of the Royal Academy, is about the same age. His wife is ft sister of Lady Berne-Jones, and aunt to Mr. Rudyard ipling. _ The " Thunderer."—lt was the splashing of two

ladle; with mud, by •an ill- behaved boi•seman,-that gave to the " Times" newspaper its nick-name the Thnnderer. There was then, as th.re is now, nothing unusual in women being splashed with road mud, but it was said on this occasion, that the rider was the Duke of Cumhorland,and it was that assertion which produced in the "Times " a very wrathful article on the rudeness of the horseman. The rider however, was not the Duke of Cumberland, and by way of apology another article appeared in the" Times " which began " We thundered out." Pedestrians often do the same in these days

ien -iiii-e—ex-oeption, rather than the rule, fiir ' motorists to show them any courtesy on the highways - but we are a long-suffering people, and protest in such cases appear to be useless. 7 Old Bibles—After the Great Fire of London: the King's Printing Office stood on the site of the "Times" Buildings, and it was there that John Baskett printed 4 the famous " Vinegar Bible," so called from its mis- print—the parable of the vinegar (vineyard). There I used to he a p6pular belief that a guinea was offered to anyone who could find a misprint in the Bible, and there are many old vers ions in the Scriptures in which these occur. There is the " Breeches " Bible—so called from - 1 its peculiar renderings of Gen. 111. 7. Other instances are found in the " Printers " Bible—the " printers " (princes) have persecuted me ; and in the " Place- ' makers " Bible—blessed are the placemakers (peace- makers).The"Wooden Leg" Testament is'so named from the front is piece depicting Satan with a wooden leg. Princess Beatrice to Wed a Spanish Prince.—The engagement of Princess Beatrice of Saxe Coburg Gotha to Prince Alfonso of Burlion-Orleans, forms another matrimonial link between our Royal Family and Spain. The Prince, who spent some time with Princess Beatrice London last autumn, is a cousin of the King of Spain,. is the son of the Infanta Eulalie, who is well-known this conntry. Princess Beatrice, the youngest of tour daughters of the late Duke of Saxe Coburg Mite of Edinburgh) is nearly twenty-four, and some • two, or three years older than her betrothed. This • charming Princess has spent more of her time in this COflotry than her sisters, the Crown Princess of 40umania, the Grand Duchess Cyril of Russia, and the Hereditary Princess of Hohenlohe-Langenburg, and among her intimate friends here are Mrs. Spender Clay, and Mrs. Wilfred Ashley.. 4rrlvlng too late to be Married.—Formerly marriages had ordinarily to be solemnized by mid-day, but although the Aot of 1886 extended the time to 3 o'clock in the afternoon, it is occasionally found that wedding parties arrive too late, and the ceremony has to be postponed until the following day. An instance occurred the other day at Pontypridd, owing it seems i to the eccentric driving of the coachman who, it was explained, had driven another wedding party earlier in I the day. But the point is, that although such mishaps have occurred on many occasions, yet people continue 1 to defer their weddings until close upon three o'clock, and if any accident delays them, they are in danger of being too late, causing considerable inconvenience to all concerned. The practice is, at least, an unwise one, as regrettable as that of throwing confetti, which seems to he regarded by many people as an essential accom- paniment of a wedding. The London Season.—lt has been said that the coming London season is likely to be the best we have had for several years past, but what ground such OPtimists have to go upon, it would be hard to deter- mine. For instance, several hostesses who used to give big balls, have fallen out of the ranks, and there do not appear to be others forthcoming to take their Place. Some plead that nowadays it is next to impos- sible to get men for a ball, and that even when captured, they only dance when compelled to do so. Others maintain that in these extravagant times only tnillionairs can afford to give balls, dinners, receptions, concerts, etc., that the floral decorations cost a fortune, and the food fabulous sums. So many of these people, Who, in years gone by would have done their fair share entertaining slip away, and enjoy the•spring in aunDier climes' or go tours in their motor cars, and When forced to entertain simply whisk their friends off to a fashionable restaurant. And all this is bad hearing I for the debutante ! Concerning Millinery.—Fashionable milliners' show- rooms, in those instances where the sales have ceased and peace reigns once more are a curious medley, for side by side with headgear more, for wear at tee present time in this country, are the most gossamer. like creations, destined for Egypt and the Riviera. The latter make us shiver, it is true, yet we should do well to take note of these airy-fairy models, as in all probability they represent our spring modes. Violent Extremes.—The latest Parisian modistes, in connection with the clinging skirt is the abolition of petticoats, their place being taken by maillots, which are nothing more or less than "tights." Save in the case of a few extremists, the idea is not likely to "catch on "in this country, which is well, for not one woman in a hundred could wear gracefully a gown unsupported by a petticoat. Possibly it will not be long now before we tire of the cat-skin robe, which necessitates walk lug with rigid knees, and causes con- siderable discomfort in sitting down' and getting up again. Anyway there are rumours to the effect that with the late spring, we shall have our pretty full skirts—so infinitely more graceful and charming ein every way—back once more. Hair Ornaments for the Afternoon.—Many leading dressmakers have for some time past supplied hair ornaments with certain of the toilettes, maintaining, and no doubt correctly, that a gown is often spoiled by an inharmonious or undesirable headdress. And with smart afternoon frocks they are now sending home fascinating little bows, to be worn in the hair, built of velvet ribbon, the reverse side being sewn over closely with minute paste beads. The bows are wired in such a manner that glimpses of the bead "lining "—which reflects every ray of artificial light—are caught as the wearer moves. Pantomime Excursions.—Every year some addition to the attractions of the leading pantomimes, and the latest thing in pleasing trips is the running of long distance pantomime excursions, from various provincial centres to London. These offer the most comfortable conditions possible for the journey and one inclusive charge covers the cost of railway ticket, a reserved seat for the Dury-lane pantomine in London, and in addition provides dinner on the train on the up journey and supper on the return trip. Arrangements have been I made to run these pantomime specials from various 1 centres, including Manchester, Liverpool, Bristol, Cardiff, Bradford, Sheffield and other large cities, and they seem likely to be well patronised by pantomime lovers in the provinces. Fits of coughing that follow on s tickling in the throat, huskiness and tightness in the regikt of the breastbone, indicate one of the worse forms of bronchitis ; sudden difficulty in breathing in association with a cough, points to the appearance of asthma. WOULD NOT WORK.

Six Months for Neglect _ of Childien.

At the Borough Police Court on Monday, before the Mayor and other magistrates, George Harrison, Cropper's-brow, was charged with wilfully neglecting his three children in a manner likely to cause them unnecessary suffering and injury to health. Mr. A. Browne, who prosecuted on behalf of the N.S.P.C.C., said the prisoner had had three months for for the same offence. The officer of the Workhouse had told him that he had known the prisoner for the past few years and the children had been constantly going in and out of the Workhouse. Prisoner was a puddler's labourer and could earn ss, a day if he worked but he would not work. He had been married seven months, and it was his second marriage. His second wife was a native of Rotherham and her father was a very respect- aple man. When the prisoner got to know the position of his wife's father be left his work and had not worked since, last October. A Mrs. Steel had been looking after the children, as had also a Mrs. Shaw,who were very kind and charitable to them. They had con- stantly fed the children. When Inspector Cox visited the children he found them in a very neglectful condi- tion, being inadequately clothed and ravenously hungry. Had it not been for the kindness of the neighbours the children would have been starved to death. Inspector Cox Of the N.S.P.C.C. said he had known prisoner for the last three years. He was a regular scamp and quite worthless. He had visited him at his lodgings four times and warned him on several occasions. He would not work or maintain his family. On the 10th December he (the Inspector) took the children to the Workhouse himself. _ . .

- Mrs. Steel, 62, Albin-street, said the prisoner was her brother-in-law by his first marriage. Mrs. Shaw and herself had fed the children every time they came to their houses. He would not work.

Mrs. Harrison,the prisoner's wife, and who is 21years of age' was then called but said she had nothing to say. The Chief Constable said the prisoner had been nine times previously convicted, the last time being in December me al, en ho was soot to gaol for two° months.

The prisoner said he hoped the bench would give him less this time. The Mayor said it did not take the bench long to deal with a " brute like prisoner." lie would have to go to gaol for bix months with hard labour.

At nine-thirty on the following morning, Jako Potter, with the cruel consciousness of a thick and swelled head strong upon him, for Ifs meet- ing with Pete had led to very late hours and un- limited rums hot, turned over on his pillow, and reached for the boll rope. "Morning, Gibbings," he remarked, with con- descending familivity, as the " boots " intruded his white head. "Is Mr. Smith, of London—No. 15—up yet?"

I " 'e got hup two hours since." "Not skipped. has he?" ejaculated Potter, breathlessly, jumping out of bed and hurriedly commencing to dress,

"No, sir, responded Gibbings, struggling to keep a solemn face. 'E ain't likely to go in any great 'urry, not 'e." Ask him to step up here for a minute or two, if he will please oblige me." " Yessir." And Gibbings departed. Almost the next moment . there came the sound of quick footsteps. The bedroom door was flung open and the remorseless Potter's un- fortunate victim entered. He was fully and even fashionably dressed as though for some function.

"You want me?" began "Mr. Smith," brusquely. " Yes—to part with a very much bigger quantity of dust than you did last night," answered Potter, coolly, swinging his braces ovqr his shoulders and catching them in front. "If not, I'll--" But the threat was never uttered. "Smith" had him by the throat and hurled him back- ward on to the bed. "You reptile!" he hissed. "You haden't even the dirty honour of a thief! Those notes— quick!—where are they? I'll choke you like dog ! " His grip tightened ominously. Potter squirmed helpless for a second or two, the while his face grew red and swollen. He tried to cry out for help. But the effort was vain. So with quivering fingers he pointed to the pillow behind him. His assailant threw the pillow aside, and took up the fat pocket-book below it. Then, extracting the rustling flimsie% it contained, he flung the empty case contemptu- ously to the floor and strode out of the room, without a second glance at the wretched Potter. WOMAN'S- REALM.

FOR EVENING WEAR.

Smart evening toilets' worn during the corning winter season will have no sleeves to boast of, jewelled lace straps or straps of beauti- ful embroideries taking their place. Floating sleeves of fine tulle often fall from these straps, outlining the arm, and so making an artistic effect. loth for day and evening wear skirts are much tighter and made to fit closely in front, while the chief trimming of these new princess skirts is a band of jewelled embroidery touching the hem. A NOVEL PARTY.

This is pre -eminently a children's winter. While those of a larger growth are grumbling at the general dulness and complaining of the scarcity of social entertainments, there is a per- fect boom in juvenile parties. The fact is, hostesses find it a much more thankful task to entertain the youngsters, who are obviously pleased, and frankly come to enjoy themselves; moreover, the guests are all glad to meet each other. I have an idea, writes "Ambrosia" in the World, that an entertainment for " grown- ups " planned on the lines of a juvenile party would prove a huge success. The guests should be kept in ignorance of the nature of the enter- tainment until they arrived, or they would spoil its character by assuming juvenility in advance. The plan would be to tell them nothing before- hand, and simply leave those who would not " play " to their own devices. I feel sure the hostess who would have the courage to do it would score a success.

===== 0002601_19080118_art0033.txt | included=true | included =====
A SIMPLE BUT EFIrECTIVE GOWN.

Illustrated in our sketch is a simple but most effective gown, suitable for every-day afternoon wear. A very soft cashmere in an exquisite shade of bat's-wing grey is the material chosen for the realisation of this charming design, the only trimming being a narrow tarnished silver galon and a little pleated grey silk. The bodice is arranged in pinafore style, but is treated in rather an original fashion, the front being ar- ranged in a sort of plastron, which widens out over the shoulders. This plastron is trimmed

with the silver galon, arranged in a band placed about half an inch fcom the edge, which runs all round the pl:istron, and some tarnished silver buttons, and is bordered by a tight little pleating of the grey silk. The sleeves are simple three. quarter affairs, terminating in a braided and frilled cuff which matches the corsage. Inside the pinafore comes a veBt of tucked white net, the close-fitting under-sleeves being made of the same dainty material. he skirt is perfectly plain, cut very long, and slightly trainediat the 'back.

HOW T 4 KEEP YOUNG.

Someone once asked a Woman how it was she kept her youth so wonderfully. Her hair was snowy white, she wa6 eighty years old, and her enprgy was waning, but she never impressed one with the idea of age, for her heart was still young in sympathy and interests. And this was her answer: "I knew hqw to forget disagreeable things. I tried to master the art of saying pleasant things. I did not expect too much of my friends. I kept my nerves well in hand, and did not allow them to bore other people. I tried to find any work that came to hand congenial."

TO-DAY'S RECIPE.

COCOANIIT-RICE MOULDS.—HaIf an ounce of gelatine, three ounces of rice, two ounces of grated cocoanut, one and a-quarter pint of milk, two ounces of loaf sugar, one gill of cream. Put the gelatine into a stewpan with enough milk to cover it, and diEeolve it slowly over a mild heat. Boil the rest of the milk with the rice until quite tender, then add the sugar and the cocoanut, and strain in the dissolved gelatine. Whip the cream, and when the rice is cool mix lightly together. Have some small moulds masked with lemon jelly. rour tne rice mixture into the prepared moulds. Put a thin layer of jelly on the top of each, and put on the ice to eet. Turn out on to a dish and serve pith chopped itUz arranged round,

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

Colborne-street Baptist Church. Dr. J. G. Marshallol.A., principal of the Baptist College, Manchester, was the preacher at this place of worship on Sunday, and delivered two eloquent and powerful , sermons. In the afternoon Mr. Marshall distributed the prizes to the Bible class and in his address gave some valuable advice. The collectirs were in aid of Baptist College fund. There were good congregations throughout the day.

===== 0002601_19080118_art0037.txt | included=true | included =====
St. James', Latchford.

The Vicar, writing in "St. James' Latchford, Parish Magazine," says : Since writing my last letter to this Magazine I have offered the second curacy to Mr. E. M. Swift, of the Royal University of Ireland. It is not probable that Mr. Swift will be ordained deacon until June or even September, and so some time must elapse before we can hope to have him a member of our clerical staff, but I have every reason to believe that when he comes we shall find him of great help."

===== 0002601_19080118_art0038.txt | included=true | included =====
St Peter's Church.

The members of the Mothers' Meeting in connection with this Church held their annual tea party on Monday evening. The mothers and friends assembled to the number of 125, and an excellent tea was provided for them, followed by an enjoyable entertainment. At the close of the gathering over a hundred useful garments were distribuied by Mrs. Percival. They had been kindly given by Lady Greenall, Miss Comber and the Warrington and South Lancashire Needlework Guild. Oa Tuesday evening the Young Women's Bible Class tea and conversazione were held. The following ladies and gentlemen acted as entertainers :—The Misses M. Brundrett, Forshaw, Dollie Percival, Louie Bennie, Mr. Morris, and Masters Jack Greaves and Young. The accompanists were Mr. F. Webster and Mrs. Greaves. Speeches were delivered by the Vicar,the Churcl warden, Mr. Wrattislaw and Captain Lund.

===== 0002601_19080118_art0039.txt | included=true | included =====
Free Church Council—Week of Prayer.

The meetings for the week of prayer as arranged by the National Free Church Council were brought to a close locally on Saturday evening last when the gathering took place at the independent Methodist Chapel, Friars Green. Mr. Wynne Jones presided, (in the absence through illness of the Rev. F. Vaughan) and in expressing the sympathy of the meet- ing with lkfr. Vaughan in his affliction hoped that he would soon he restored to health. The Rev. JoriN YONGE afterwards addressed the meeting, and spoke of the importance of greater concentration of effort in the work of the Free Churches of Warrington as well as an increase of spirituality with individual members. On Sunday the annual interchange of prlpits took place, and the services at the different churches were characterized by great heartiness. After the church services a united communion service was held in the Wycliffe Congregational Church. The Rev. John Yonge presided over a good congregation, and said they had met together in the name of Christ, in mutual love and confidence in one another. He hoped the Free Churches would have a happy New Year in united ser- vice for Christ. The Rev. T. Itlellor gave the com- munion address. He said that in a service like that they should remember that they were not there to ; merely partake of the bread and wine, but to realize the significance of those elements. Spiritual things ! must be spiritually discerned, and the great lesson of such a feast as that was that they should recognise the spiritual significance of the bread and wine which !stood for the body and blood of Christ. A collection , was taken to defray expenses.

===== 0002601_19080118_art0040.txt | included=true | included =====
CROSFIELD'S DRAMATIC SOCIETY.

This Society continues to make encouraging progress, and the members are to he congratulated upon the success of the opening annual performances wbich took place in the works dining hall on Thursday evening. There was a large audimma, nun the entertainment gave vCly manifest pleasure. Variety was the keynote of the programme, no fewer than three short plays being presented. The first was the comedy "My Preserver,' in which Mr. F. B. Gartland as Enoch Mailable, Esq., an old bachelor took first honours. Miss L. Cank also acted very naturally as Dolly, a country girl, and Mr. G. B. Dean was highly entertaining as Bilberry, a plough boy. The other parts were well sustained by the following :—William Bury (a pro- fessional " contributor ") Mr. S. F. Welsby ; Dr. Pals- ford (a medical man and friend of Mailable) Mr. T. Lawton ; Philip (Mallable's footman), Mr. H. Flood ; Estelle Fitzwater (Niallable's ward)' Miss E. Wright ; Mary Walker (her maid), Miss S. Derbyshire ; Mrs. Banter (a widow), Miss A. Kinsey ; Miss Banter (her daughter), Miss A. Rowe. This play was followed by another comedy" Anything for a change" which proved immensely popular. Miss L. Hewitt, as Margaret Honeyball, acted well and delighted all with her sweet singing. Mr. W. Tynan and Mr. G. B. Dean did excellently in the roles of Paul Honeyball and Mr. Swoppington. The other characters were credit- ably taken by :—Jeremy Census (registrar of births, deaths, tto.), Mr. J. Morris ; Mrs. Honeyball (wife of Paul), Miss Rigby; Eliza (servant to Mrs. Honeyball), ' Miss A. Rowe ; The entertainment concluded with the farce "Cherry bounce," and the ridiculous complications created by the pranks of the diligent servant created laughter loud and long. Mr. F. E. Gartland was a capital Mr. Oldrents and Mr. H. Flood rose to the occasion as Gregory Homespun his much-abused man. Miss L. Cank acquitted herself admirably as Mrs. Thompson. The remainder of the cast was as follows :--Gammon (a farmer and tenant of .Mr. Oldrents), Mr. J. Morris ; Spinage (a farmer and tenant of Mr. Oldrents), Mr. NV. 11. Tomkinson ; Doctor's boy, Mr. H. Griffiths. The works orchestra was in attendance and added much to the evening's enjoyment with the selections "A conntry girl " (Monckton), "La Glpsy kfaznrka " (Gonne), 'Wee Macreegor "Scotch Patrol (Arno.* and" El Capstan" mach (Sousa). Great praise is due to Mr. F. J. Lord who engineered the performances and ably conduoted the orchestra.

I lost my appetite, had no energy for anything, and my weakness was dreadful. Early in 1901 I was invalided to Eng- land for a change. There are abundant signs THE COMING of the approaching session SESSION. of Parliament, the course of which may be predicted with more than the usual certainty. The Govern_ ment, it seems clear, will not encumber itself with ' a large number of bills. There will be three measures of the first importance, dealing with Education, Licensing, and Old-Age Pensions. Mr. Asounu is a man of stout heart, and he may be depended upon to face the financial situation with spirit and confidence ; but he is not a Chancellor of the Exchequer whom any reasonable person will envy. After tending steadily upwards for a long time past, the revenue is again on the decline, not even the astounding Limerick craze having made up for the diminished frequency of the deaths of millionaires, though th 3 Post Office is the only one of the Departments which will be able to show a profit in excess of expectations. On the one hand Mr. AsQuITH has to provide Old-Age Pensions, and on the other he has to look forward to a probable increase in the demands of the Navy. We were never, as Sir JOHN Fteuxx has assured us, in such a state of naval preparedness as at present, and yet we are obviously on the threshold of a panic, with Mr. STEAD endeavouring to repeat his former success- ful naval scare and an Opposition willing and ready to make use of this o-.• any other weapon to damage the Government. A safe policy for the CHANCELLOR of the EXCHEQUER in this respect might be based on the old saying that enough is as good as a feast. Is Sir JOHN FIsHER'S statement to be abso- lately relied upon ? In that case we are safe against all possible attack, and we do not require to be more than safe. But should the shipbuilding of Germany, or any other country, threaten or endanger our security, then we must be prepared to submit to whatever financial burden may be laid upon us as a means of maintaining our superiority at sea. RICHARD COBDEN, who is now so often scornfully mentioned by thus who know little about him, was as eager an advocate of a strong Navy as Lord CHARLES BERESFORD would be if he were now in Parliament, or as Mr., CARLYON BELLAIRS actually is. He would, as he said, be willing to vote money in any quantity necessary for the protection of the country and its commerce. We take it that in this matter we are all of the same mind; but we need not, therefore, lose our heads because the GERMAN Kamm has set his mind on the construction of a powerful navy. It would be years before Germany could come up to our own level, even if we did next to nothing ; and in making calculation on the two- Power basis we have to consider how very far Russia still is from the position which she occupied before the war with Japan.

Evidently, Mr. ASQUITH has not been listening to those who assure him that Old-age Pensi3ns must come solely out of incomes and the taxation of land. All classes are to contribute,which means that we must not look for any immediate lessen- ing of the burden of indirect taxation, or possibly, though not probably, that we shall have to submit to an increase. The Education Bill is to be short, simple, and drastic. Mr. Asuna's particular I mention of it suggests that this, also, may be a measure based on finance, in which case, however it may excite the anger, it will not be at the mercy of the House of Lords. On more than one occasion the CHANCELLOR of the EXCHEQUER has hintel that the Licensing Bill, of which he is himself to take charge, will contain provisions for dealing with those clubs which are not really clubs, but drinking dens. On what line will he proceed? In all probability he will confine him- self to interference with the proprietary clubs. Should that be so, he will have a strong force of 'public opinion on his side.

The contest for the repre- MR. MAGINN'S sentation of St. Austin's CANDIDATURE. Ward is proving a very keen one. Mr. Maginn is endeavouring to win the seat as an Independent, and we hope every elector who desires a represen- tative who will speak wit 4 his own voice and act on his own responsibility will plump for Mr. Maginn. There are more then enough party nominees in the Council already whose main duty seems to be—as Mr. Maginn puts it—to keep the draught off their political superiors. Judging hy the way Mr. Broadhurst, the Conservative candi- date, is being actively supported by the big wigs of the Tory party some such devoted personal service will be expected of him in the future. We wonder if it e) ccur a to the:electors to consider whether men like Sir Gilbert Greenall and Mr, W. Peter Rylands are taking part in this municipal bye-election solely in the interests of the ward. It is reported that Mr. Rylands has said he will not be content until the Tory party has a working majority of three-fifths of the Council members. This provides a reason for his intro- duction on Wednesday, of Imperialism, Foreign and Colonial policy, into a municipal contest. Mr. Broadhurst is apparently to be another pawn in the political game if he is complaisant and the

electors do not object. We trust the voters will go into the election on Monday with their eyes open and vote solidly for Mr. John Maginn, who has proved in the past his independence of action and his capacity for enterprising administration.

Mr. Stead's defection from MR. STEAD the advocacy of peace to

===== 0002601_19080118_art0054.txt | included=true | included =====
.AND PEACE.

Cita of increasing our armaments has given very great pain to his associates of former days. He has been reproved by many influential peace bodies, and the National Council of Peace SJcieties has now undertaken the task of reproving him for the statement that "The Peace Party" is even more anxious to maintain our naval suprern.tcy than the "Jingoes." After all it is only a phrase, and when the Peace Council, on the strength of containing representatives of nearly every poace organisation in the United Kingdom, declares that not one holds the opinion attributed by Mr. Stead, it surely forgets that, as matters now stand, the British Fleet is the greatest force for peace which the world has got. Is there any advocate of peace who would care to contemplate the consequences of the loss of British naval supremacy?

The Corporation is to be congratulated on having emerged with such solid advantages from the arbi- tration in respect of the famous Lawson contract. Mr. Lawson claimed £36,425 Is. from the Corporation over and above the £35,484 which the Corporation had paid to him. As a result of the arbitrator's award, Mr. Lawson's amazing claim is set on one side in its entirety, and he is further directed to pay to the Corporation the sum o f £1,432, and the costs of the arbitration. rhis is the sum and substance of the award, after pro- tracted proceedings and much legal argument ' before Mr. G. H. Hill, MICE. It will be remembered that the contract was let to Mr. George Lawson for the sinking of wells at Winwick and Delph-lane in August 1900. The work was unusually protracted and lasted over four years. Mr. Lawson's contract was for £24,400 with certain contingencies. Apparently those contingencies arose, for the Corporation paid the contractor altogether £35,484,and than found that they were a long way from being out the wood—or the water. During the time the work was proceeding very little information was given to the Council, and the fact that the Council was so frequently asked to sanction the payment of large sums of money—over and above the contract price—merely on the certificate of the engineer was the primary cause of the Non-Trading attack on the Water Committee and the memorable scenes in the Council Chamber. It seemed, that so long as contingencies arose in the shape of excessive water, the pumping and the paying were to go on indefinitely. The Water Committee signally failed to justify their position when 4uestioned in open Council and made matters worse by a policy ot concealment which engendered suspicion: How much more they would have paid to the contractor had it not been for the pertinacity of the Non-Traders nobody knows. The latter forced an investi- gation, and the decision to resort to arbitration followed. It is only on the result of that arbitration that we can congratulate the Corporation. Much of the credit for the satis- factory issue is due to the Non-Traders in the Council whose vindication for what seemed like persecution of the Water Committee, is complete. They fought unflinchingly for investigation by an independent committee, and if they had not carried the day there is no telling to what extent the town's liabilities in this matter might have swollen. After the next Council meeting we should not hear much more about the Lawson Contract. It has been a municipal nightmare for several years and everybody will be thankful when it is buried in oblivion.

NOTES AND NOTIONS.

The Veterans' Relief Fund, inaugurated by Lord Roberts, is already meeting with splendid support. Up to Thursday night the sums received totalled £11,296 Os. 71d. * * " £95,000,000 Up "is the riotously exultant heading in a morning paper over a glowing eulogy of last year's "huge increase in trade." This ought to warm the cockles of the hearts of the British workman on the Embankment these nights.—" London Opinion."

They were insurance agents, and they were boast- ing of the promptness with which their respective , companies paid claims. " You may all be slick," said one who had been listening, "but you can't equal. what we do out in the States. I know an office on the second floor of a 16-storey sky scraper. One day a workman fell from the roof to the pavement. Well, as he came past the window of the insurance office on the second floor a clerk handed him his cheque."

* * * The fight in St. Austin's Ward will be memorable for a unique election meeting. On Thursday the singular spectacle of the rival candidates addressing the same gathering was witnessed and the crowded audience seemed to enjoy it. , Although the meeting was called by Mr. Magian, Mr. Broadhurst on entering was at once escorted to a seat near the platform and early in the meeting was accorded the unusual privilege of delivering an address on a warmly dis- puted point. Matters however, soon got lively and the Conservative candidate, .apparently on the horns of a dilemma when Mr. Magmn pressed for details, deemed it expedient to beat 3 dignified retreat. * * * Another case of the biter bitten ! At Tuesday evening's meeting in support of Mr. Maginn's candi- didature an emissary of the opposition triumphantly asked if it was not the fact that whilst he was in the ' Council Mr. Magian opposed an increase of wcges for Corporation labourers. Mr. Maginn had, however, got wind of this attempt to drag a red herring across the trail, and his questioner collapsed on hearing that Mr. Magnin did not vote at all on the question of a minumum wage of 215., whilst those who opposed the the attempt to discrealt Mr. Maguan;was thus clearly resolution included such Tory stalwarts as Councillors Sankey, Hulse and Fulton-Smith. The hollowness of demonstrated.

* * * The contrast in platforms at the election meetings in St. Austin's Ward has been so marked as to call forth much comment. At the Conservative meeting on Wednesday evening Mr. Broadhurst!s supporters were practically all "swells," and many people were asking why these distinguished ornaments of the Tory party should be so deeply concerned about the affairs of St. Austin's Ward and be anxious that it should be represented by a workingman. At Mr. Maginn's meeting the following evening there was none of this display of wealth and influence—in fact, one of the speakers came straight from his work at the forge and addressed the gathering in his working clothes. The audience seemed to appreciate the homely remarks from a fellow-toiler.

* * * It is worth remembering to-day that Mr. Broadhurst was a candidate for municipal honours last February, when he opposed Councillor Hawthorn in Whitecross Ward. On that occasion his employer, Mr. F. Monks, had something interesting to say about his candidature. Speaking about the need for business men in the Council Mr. Monks said " He was surprised to-day to find that the latest candidate was a blacksmith in his own works. He did not know that he (the candidate) contributed very much to the rates but he knew that the firm did, and that it was practically coming to this —that their own workmen were going to say how much rates they would have to pay. Whether that was .a satisfactory state of things or not he would leave his hearers to judge."

* * * Life is made up of marble and mud.—/lawthorne. He jests at scares who never dodged a car.—Mizmer Some want a fortune to make them gentlemen.— La 'Tis a hard thing for a man to be both popular and virtuous.— Seneca. There is always somebody to believe in anyone who is uppermost. —Beecher. He that does good works for praise sells an in- estimable jewel for a trifte.—St..Gregory. Curiosity after the affairs of others cannot be with- out envy and an evil mind.—Jeremy Taylor. A man with a career can have no time to waste upon his wife or his friends—he has to devote it wholly to his enemies.—Mrs. Craigie,

Our neutral contemporary, the "Guardian," seems to have been taken under the sheltering wing of Sir Gilbert and Lady Greenall, who have made one or or two visits just recently in order to assist their protege in a little self-glorification. The other day her ladyship was invited to start a new machine, which was afterwards christened "Lady Greenall." We suppose, ere long, some inky-handed "printer's devil" will have to go round and oil" Lady Greenall " !

* * * "That wretched damnable brook" was Mr. John Maginn's way of alluding to the pellucid stream which meanders through Sankey Bridges distributing foul odours along its course. We hope the brook will feel as much better as he apparently did after getting the adjective off his mind. Then there is the eaually " damnable " bridge and the canal and other" damn- able " things. In faet, of Sankey Bridges, one might say with Macbeth" Out damned spot !"

* * * Th Warrington Parliamentary Debating Society has at last been successfully launched and if the present enthusiasm continues there should be some exciting and illuminating discussions. Liberals are reminded that they will be called upon to form the first Government and with the object of selecting a Premier a meeting will be held at Atkinson's Cafe next Tuesday evening. A. large attendance is requested. * * * The question of infantile mortality in]Warrington is not being allowed to rest. An influential deputa- tion of ladies recently waited upon the Mayor and the Chairman of the Health Committee and subsequently, we understand, embodied their views in a written statement which has been forwarded to the Com- mittee. We hope the outcome will be the formation of some voluntary agency to work in conjunction with the authorities to dispel the ignorance which unfortunately so largely prevails. The many friends of Mr. W. W. Yates, an old Warringtorean and a well-known journalist, will be interested in the following which appeared in last ..4aturday's "Dewsbury Reporter" :—" Just fifty years have elapsed since the advent into journalism of Mr. W. W. Yates, who for thirty-six years ending June, 1907, was, as is well-known, the head of the " Reporter" staff. He, however, has not yet attained his professional jubilee, for the reason that for ten months in 1860-61 he was a commercial traveller for a firm of flint-glass manufacturers in St. Helens, and covered much ground in this country and Ireland. In these said-to-be " too-old-at-forty " days, it is worthy of mention that Mr. Yates, who is still in harness— light harness—commenced his business caroer in the autumn of 1843—upwards of sixty-four years ago."

* * Mr. Henry Fielding Dickens, K.C., entered upon his sixtieth year yesterday. He is the sixth son of the famous novelist, and the latter at first thought of naming him Oliver Goldsmith. Dickens certainly paid tribute to literature in the names of sons. One was named Walter Landor, another Francis Jeffrey, a third Alfred Tennyson, a fourth Sydney Smith Haldimand, and a fifth Edward Bulwer Lytton. Henry Fielding was something of a favourite with his father who was particularly pleased when he gained a scholarship at Cambridge. Mr. Dickens, KC., has a son who is a barrister, and nearly a year ago father and son appeared as opposing council in a trial for theft at Chatham. The piquancy of the situation was enhanced by sundry arguments between the two. * * * The Army is still suffering from a redundant supply of colonels. In 1906 the authorities decided that the establishment of colonels should be fixed at 350, and it was understood that steps were to be taken to reduce it to that number. Unfortunately, nothing was done, I and the consequence is that there is still a large body of colonels not only unemployed, but without the pros- pect of employment. It is understood that Mr. Hal- dane and the Army Council are devoting their atten- tion to the matter, and that they will endeavour to make the decision of 1906 effective. * * *•

One of the difficulties attendant upon the mother of a large family is keeping count, if we are to judge by what was heard at the Wakefield County Court on Tuesday. Judge Greenhow asked: Now, can you tell me your family? Can you remember so many of them? (Laughter.) Married Woman : I can tell you all. There are three boys working, a girl in place, one married, and five others. His Honour: Have you only had 10 altogether? The Wife : I have had 10. (Laughter.) His Honour : What has become of the other &ix ? The Wife : I have buried six, and I have 11 living. (Laughter.) His Honour : That is 17. The Wife : I have had eight boys and eight girls. His Honour gave the puzzle up. * *

The appearance of the Rev. J. R. Campbell's latest book, " Christianity and the Social Order, has called forth such a sheaf of adverse criticisms that it might have been eXpected the orator of the City Temple would have something to say in his own defence during the course of Thursday's midday sermon. A sprinkling of journalists was therefore to be seen in the congregation, but the preacher rose superior to the occasion. His discourse contained no reference to the controversy of which he is the centre, its theology not being of a startling character, and his only reference to the social problem was the eloquent peroration in which he condemned the "sickening cant" which ! says that the 100,000 fallen women of London are necessary and at the same time condemns them to social ostracism. He attributed their existence to the subjection of women by men, and his sentiments were I greeted with applause.

* * * One of the numerous practical improvements advocated by Mr. John Magian in the course of the election contest in St. Austin's Ward is the necessity for a public waiting room at the Sankey Bridges tram terminus. Most people who use the trains much will say " hear, hear " to this proposal. It has often struck us that waiting-rooms could be very easily provided at different important points. For instance, a shelter round the big lamp at the Latchford terminus would be an inestimable boon. The same thing could be done at Stockton Heath. But most urgently of all is a shelter required at Rylands-street. If a glass verandah something like those outside the theatres were projected from the adjacent walls it would go a long way towards alleviating the discomforts which many people have to endure every day in wet weather. It is time the Tramways Committee showed more enterprise and consideration for their numerous patrons. * * The theatre disasters a Barnsley and in Pennsyl- vania will doubtless call attention, both in England and the States,t o the conditions of entrance and exits to public halls. It would be better, probably, if there were some general authority to enforce the lessons of I experience over the country as a whole, and not only I locally after a disaster. The example of the railways I • illustrates this. There is rarely an accident the cause of which is discovered that does not lead to some recommendation and often to a compulsory regulation. For instance, the rule that platforms must now be built I sloping from the face instead of to it, as formerly, is a' direct result of the accident at Wellingborough, where a luggage truck ran down and dropped on to the line immediately in front of an express. There is one rule that not infrequently leads to friction between passer gers and officials. Passengers are not allowed to travel in a compartment immediately following an engine. With a full train and a holiday crowd the sight of the first two compart- ments empty has led to many an angry word and sometimes to blows. Of coarse it only occurs when there is no van next to the engine, but this appears to happen more often at holidays than at other times. %:1

Skaters have reason to feel pleased with the weather conditions, so far, of 1908. There have been two frosts and two week-ends of skating. The thaw kindly confined itself to mid-week when ordinary mortals have to work. Warrington is rather fortunate in its facilities for ice-exercise. There are the flooded meadows at Longford and the Twiggery, the Ackers Pits at Latchford, the old canal at Walton, and the dam at Lymm. The ice was of excellent quality and provided the best sport of recent years. It is a curious fact that many young people have not, prior to this season, learned to skate, though they have been anxious to do so. In the last ten years there has been very little opportunity, the spells of frost having been so brief and unreliable. It is quite likely that there will be more skating this winter, for the coldest weather is often experienced in February or early March. While the skaters are happy the poor are, of course, suffering hardships. If only coal were cheaper both rich and poor alike might view the healthy spells of dry frost with satisfaction. -

One of the kitchen maids from Beeehwood, the residence of Mr. F. L. Steel, was leaving the Dam about nine o'clock in the evening when she stepped from some planks which had been put over a danger- ous place near the side where the ice had given way, to allow others to pass. An exhibition of a particularly interesting character was opened in St. George's Mission on Thursday afternoon and will continue until to-night. It corn. Prises the collection of curios got together by the -' Society for the Propagation of the Gospel to familiarise People with the customs and particularly with the religions habits in foreign lands. There are separate stands devoted to exhibits from Borneo, Australia, South Africa, Central Africa, Japan, India and China, .R addition to a literature stall. It had been announced that the Rev. He explained that the exhibition had now got to be pretty well known all over England as the S.P.G. Travelling Exhibition. It travelled in the different dioceses and last year he conducted 93 exhi- bitions on 93 days in five or six dioceses. The curios had been collected from all parts of the world and especially the parts where the S.P.G. bad its workers. On the stalls were articles in daily use by -the natives, ranging in variety from garments to idols. RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

RUNCORN CO-OPERATIVE SOCIETY.

During the past .half-year the turnover of the Runcorn and Widnes Co-operative Society was £109,351, an increase of £14,600 over the corresponding period. The trade for the year was by far the largest total recorded by the society—namely, £22l,9oo—and there was distributed in dividend and interest £32,850.

NEW MINISTER FOR RUNCORN. The Rev. Joseph Miller, of Regent-street Congrega- tional Church, Oldham, has accepted a unanimous invi- tation from Bethesda Congregational Church, Runcorn, and will commence his ministry there on the first Sunday in February. Trained at Nottingham Theo- logical College, Mr. Miller went to Oldham three years ago, and his Rork has met with great approval.

RUNCORN BARGENIAN MISSING.

John Whitney, a native of Runcorn, recently residing in Manchester, has been missing since New Year's Day.. He was captain of a Shin Canal Company's barge, and on that day his vessel was in the Duke's Dock, Liver- pool. Runcorn Grappling Corps have dragged the docks at the South-end of Liverpool without result. Whitney is sixty-four years of age.

TETE " CAT " IN THE PANTOMME. . A WIDNES BOY'S OPPORTUNITY.

A discussion took Pl-aCa -at- -S-t.-1-4ele-tis-iolice Court on Monday, when Mr. James Kiddie applied to the magistrates for a licence permitting James Percival, aged twelve, a Widnes boy, to perform in a pantomime at the Hippodrome. He stated that the boy would he on the stage at intervals from six o'clock to 10.45 p.m. He was sent to the Catholic school in every town they visited. He would be sent to school that afternoon. The Clerk : He will go to school to-morrow at nine? Mr. Kiddie: Yes. The Clerk : So the boy is twelve years old, and he will have to get up at eight o'clock in the morning and go on working after school, and he won't get to bed until half-past eleven. Mr, Kiddie : Oh, before that. He will be in bed by eleven. The Clerk : Well it is ridiculous. What are the. boy's parents? Mr. Kiddie: They are poor people. The Clerk : How long have you had this boy at this job? Mr. Kiddie: Three weeks to-day. He is playing the "cat" in the pantomime "Dick Whittington. l'he boy was called forward, and said his father was dead. He had one brother, aged nine, and three sisters, aged fifteen, seven, and four respectively. His uncle kept the house going, and worked in the chemical yard. He had passed standard four. Mr. Kiddie read a letter from the boy's schoolmaster, which expressed the opinion that Mr. Kiddie was doing well by the lad, and that it was a good opening for him. He would be better off than at home. The Clerk : It means he will have to sacrifice his education or the money-making. Mr. Kiddie said the tour would last until March. The bench granted the licence. WIDNES GAS PROFITS.

1 COUNCIL v. TOWN'S MEETING. The Widnes Town Council on Tuesday considered their position with regard to the disposition of the handsome profits of the gasworks. Under the new bill to be promoted next session the Corporation desire to obtain the power to allocate surplus profits to the relief of the rates. A town's meeting • a week ago declared itself strongly in favour of the existing system of using the profit to increase the works and reduce the price of gas. Councillor Lewis moved that a poll of the burgesses be taken. He said be could not accept the decision of the town's meeting as in any sense final. The decision was achieved by the whole-hearted expounding of falla- cies by gentlemen who knew better. The statement that any change in the existing system would result in the raising of the price of gas was absolutely a fallacy, and the whole question of' the price of gas was simply a matter of the Council's poiicy. Alderman George Neil seconded, remarking that it was regrettable that such a meeting as that of a week , ago should have been held. He could not call any man who attended that meeting and voted against the Bill anything but a puppet. oouncillor Smith: I was there, but lam no puppet. Alderman Neil urged that if the Council was fit to be trusted with the sewers, schools, and highways, it was fit to be trusted with the profits of the gasworks. Councillor George Davies thought it was no use spending the £lOO which a poll would mean. He was satisfied with the decision the ratepayers' meeting Rrrived at, although he believed that their action was a mistake and they had voted under compulsion—the compulsion of presence and the compulsion of the power of the eye. He would like to know who paid for the gigantic advertisement in a local paper. An official of the Corporation was credited with it, and an oppor- tunity should be afforded that official of making a dis- claimer. That advertisement called him a thief, or at any rate said he was going to be one. It was a huge lie and he threw it back with all the vehemence of his soul in the teeth of the man or men who concocted it. (Applause.) Eventually the proposer and seconder of the motion were persuaded to withdraw it, and the decision of the public meeting not to allow gas profits to be devoted to the relief of the rates bolds good. •

PENKETH.

PENKETFI PARISH COUNCIL.

The quarterly meeting of the above Council was held on Monday evening in the Wesleyan day school. Dr. Murray, presided, and there were also present : Miss Tinsley, Mr. Withenshaw, Mr. Woodward, Mr. Routledge,Mr. Ditchfield, Mr. D. Houghton and Mr. E. B. Foster (clerk).

SEWER FROM THE NEW COUNTY COUNCIL SCHOOLS IN STOCK-LANE.

IMr. Woodward proposed that the District Council be requested to cut a new sewer, as he thought the ' present one was inadequate. After Dr. Murray had explained that Mr. Newcombe, ' surveyor, was of the opinion that with a few repairs, the present sewer might be given a trial before spend- ; leg so much money on cutting a new one at a cost of ' over £lOO, it was decided to adjourn the question until the next meeting.

TIrE EARLY MORNINLI MAIL. On the proposition of Mr. Withenshaw, seconded by Mr. Woodward it was resolved that the postal authorities be asked to keep back the mail which is dis- tributed to Penketh in the morning, half an hour, so as to bring the mail from the North South and the Midlands as at present the train arrives in Warrington I half an hour after the mail is despatched for Penketh.

CROSS-COUNTRY RUNNING.

The Examiner, Saturday, January 18, 1908.

ST HELENS.

FATHER SENT TO GAOL.

- - - James Hewitt, bricksetter, was sent to prison for two months with hard labour at St. Helens, on Satur- day, for leaving his wife and four children in the Workhouse, and making no efforts to maintain them. The family had cost the guardians £7B us. Gd. INDIAN MUTINY VETERAN'S DEATH.

' William Fairclough, an old Indian Mutiny veteran, died in the St. Helens Hospital on Saturday morning after an illness which lasted six months. Deceased belonged to the 2nd 8.R.8. Ride Brigade, and served throughout the Indian Mutiny. He was presented with an Indian Mutiny medal. For the last fourteen years he had been in receipt of a pension. He was employed at Messrs. Pilkington's Works at St. Helens for thirty- eight years, working there up to two years ago. REPRESENTATION OF ST. HELENS. SIR H. SETON-KARR'S DECISION.

It is authoritatively announced that Sir Fleury Seton- Karr, C.M.G., who was for twenty years Member of Parliament for St. Helens, but who was defeated at the last general election by a Labour candidate, has decided to relinquish his claims upon the constituency. Sir Henry was first elected on November 21, 1835, by a majority of 57 over the late Sir David Gamble, and was returned on four subsequent occasions. Sir Henry is an excellent speaker, with a. very attractive manner. As a big-game shooter he is known throughout the world, and in the local Conservative Club are to be seen many trophies of his shooting expsditions—from Norway, North America, and other countries. He was also a prominent member of the Parliamentary golf team on the Conservative side. For some time an impression has got abroad that Sir Henry was about to sever his connection with the Parliamentary source of the local Conservative party, and his present decision is by no means unexpected. DEATH OF A ST. HELENS PRIEST. On Saturday morning the death took place of the Rev. Father Aloysius Guibara. S.J., one of the priests at Holy Cross Church, St. Helens. He had only been ill since the beginning of the week, the cause of death being a severe form of influenza. Father Guibara, who was 45 years of age, was born in Glasgow, but was of Spanish parentage. He was educated at Stonyhurst C'ollege, and was ordained about eleven years ogo. He was appointed spiritual director at the Jesuit College at Malta, and afterwards served on missions at Bristol and Bournemouth, being transferred to St. Helens about two years ago. He was highly esteemed throughout the parish.

THE FUNERAL. Holy Cross Church was crowded by a respectful and sorrowing congregation on Tuesday, when the funeral took place. Requiem Mass was celebrated at eleven o'clock by Father Brown, rector of the Church of the Holy Name, Manchester, assisted by Father Ward, rector of Lowe House, St. Helens, and Father Kenna, of ?rescot. The church was hung with crape and the coffin was placed in front of the high altar. The diocesan choirs of priests sang the music of the Mass. Father Sergeant was director of ceremonies, and there were present representative clergy from all parts of the diocese, as well as the local priests and ,prominent Catholics of the town. The service was attended by the members of the Men's Guild, the young men's sodality, and the boys and girls' guilds. Members of the young men's sodality acted as bearers. The route to the cemetery was lined by the townspeople, and there was every manifestion of sorrow and regard. The mourners were Mr. and Mrs. William Cantephex, London, (brother-in-law and sister), Mr. and Mrs. M‘Guinness (Liverpool), Mr. and Mrs. Poorgate (Liverpool), Miss Clara Roberts (Liver- pool). The clergy present were the Rev. Fathers Eleathcote and Kellett (Preston), Swift, Brady, and Moran (Lowe House), Coverdale and Beauclero (Portico), Dr. Bennett and Holden (Wildenshavv), Dean Powell (Birchley), Proctor, Sargeant, and Seddon (Holy Cross), Day and Nash (Liverpool), Martin, Carolan, and Lomax (Wigan), Murphy and Bowler (Sacred Heart), Kenna, Lightbound, and Toohey (Prescot), Donoghue (Haydock), O'Sullivan (Blackbrook), Carr (Rainhill), Carroll (Rainford), O'Meara and Lynch (Ashton), Riley (Leigh), Lowry (Derbyshire Hill), Bertrand and Herbert (Sutton). There also attended members of the Children of Mary, the Guild of St. Agnes, Sacred Heart Association, Young Men's Sodality, ann Boys' Guild As the coffin was borne to the grave,the choir of priests. in charge of the Rev. Father \Valmsley, sang "In Paradisum." The service at the graveside was per- formed by the Rev. Father Proctor.

EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON.

REV. E. BOWCOCK. riThe Rev. E. Bowcock (formerly Primitive Methodist Minister at Earlestown) has been ,unanimously invited for the Manchester Fourth Circuit until 1912. Mr. Bowcock has consented to stay until 1910, but has reserved his decision for the fourth and fifth years to a later date.
