LOCAL OBITUARY.

Mr. John Pattison.

IWarrington has lost its oldest male inhabitant by I the death at the age of ninety-four of Mr. John Patti- son, who passed away at the residence of his son-in- law, Mr. John Gandy, at 6.15 on Tuesday morning. "Considering his advanced age, Mr. Pattison was in ! fairly good health-up to a few weeks ago, but the cold I weather experienced of late made it necessary for him to discontinue the frequent walks which he was i accustomed to take. Shortly after Christmas his con- dition became serious and he gradually sank, the cause of death being senile decay. Mr. Pattison had had a most interestin& career. He ! was born in Sowerby, Thirsk, Yorkshire, on the 4th December, 1813, two years before the battle of I Waterloo, and was, therefore, in his 05th year. When , a young man he was apprenticed to a Mr. Squires, a tanner at St. Helens, with whom he remained several I years, subsequently accompanying him to Warrington, I where the business was continued in Mersey-street. , Leaving tanning, Mr. Pattison became a grocer and i bread dealer in Mersey-street, and subsequently in ! Church-street, where he remained until he retired , ' from business altogether. He was an ardent sup- I 1 porter of the Wesleyan Church, and worshipped in i the old chapel in Bank-street, which was subsequently lused as a County Court, and is now the home of the , Ragged School and Missimit At that time Warring- ton and Runcorn formed one circuit, and three ministers were engaged. His interest in the Wesleyan Church continuti up to the time of his death and he was a familial figure at church gatherings. Mr. Pattison was one of those who walked the borough boundary before the election of Mr. William Beamont 'as first Mayor of Warrington. He witnessed many I great changes in the town and could remember scores of fields now covered with shops, works, and dwelling- houses. He had a vivid recollection of the celebra- tion 3 in Warrington of the Coronation of the late , Queen Victoria, and he could vouch for ' the interesting fact that at one time no fewer , than 22 four-horse coaches ran through Warrington every day plying between London, Edin- burgh, Manchester, Liverpool, and other places. The principal calling places for rest and refreshment were the Lion Hotel in Bridge-street and the George Hotel in Sankey-street. While Mr. Pattison was serving his apprenticeship in Yorkshire the luxury of gas and candles was entirely out of the question, in his case at ! any rate, and he said that when it was dark he had I either to go to bed or ramble about the streets. Read- ing was quite an impossibility, and if he Iwished to strike a light he had to resort to the old- fashioned flint. When he first remembered Warring- ton there were no police and the night-watchmen might be heard at all hours of the night calling out the , time.

Mr. Pattison was the father of the celebrated com- poser Mr. T. Mee Pattison, of London, who was at one time a very prominent musician in Warrington. He attributed his great longevity, among other things, to having lived the "simple life," both in the matter of food and drink. He was not a total abstainer, but he never smoked, and greatly benefited by the habit of taking long walking exercise whenever the state of the weather irriaitted and his own health was sufficiently good. He had made a large circle of friends by his pleasant and kindly disposition, and his death has occasioned deep regret. THE FUNERAL. At the funeral which took place at the Cemetery yesterday afternoon many townspeople attended to pay their last respects to one who had so long been a familiar figure in Warrington. The first part of the service was held in the Bold-street Wesleyan Church. The Rev. John Grinnell read the Psalm and the Rev. T. Miller read the lesson end offered prayer. The hymns "Rock of Ages " and " Give me the wings of faith " were sung. Mr. Frank Hickman presided at the organ and played "0 rest in the Lord " and the " Dead March"in Saati.The service at the graveside was conducted bf the Rev. T. Miller and the Revs. J. Gunnell and W. Mellor were also present. The chief mourners were : Mr. T. Mee Pattison, London, (son), Mr. J. Gandy (son-in-law), Mr. J. T. Pattison and Mr. Walter Gandy (grandson), and Mr. Richard Swift. Amongst other present were Messrs. James Parkinson, George Weaver and Doncaster (Wesleyan Society Stewards), Alderman Bennett, Councillor 'Sankey, Mr. David Garnett, and Mr. Levi Richardson. Wreaths were sent by Mr. and Mrs. Gandy and family, Mr. J. T. Pattison, Mrs. Sankey, and Mrs. Baines.

Miss Willis.

It is with sincere regret that we record the death of Miss Cicely D'Anyers Willis, only daughter of the Rev. Canon Willis (Rector of Warrington) and Mrs. Willis, who passed away at -the Rectory on Thursday morning after a brief illness. The deceased lady, who was twenty-six years of age, visited Dover about Christmas time to meet her brother who was coming home, and on returning she was seized with illness which developed into typhoid fever. Under Dr. Langdale's care she appeared to be progressing favourably, but she suddenly relapsed and on Wednes- day it was realized that her condition was very critical. A painful sensation was created on Thurs- day when the news of her death became generally known, for her pleasant and sympathetic disposition had gained for her a warm regard amongst a very wide circle of friends. She took a deep interest in the work of her father's parish, and was always a welcome visitor to the homes,pf the rarishioners, by whom she will be greatlf missed. The deepest sympathy will go forth to the Rector and Mrs. Willis in their great sorrow. The funeral will take place on Monday afternoon at the Warrington Cemetery. The first part of the service will be held in the Parish Church at one o'clock.

===== 0002601_19080118_art0068.txt | included=true | included =====
PARLIAMENTARY DEBATING SOCIETY.

The Second Meeting.

The adjourned inaugural business meeting of this Society was held in the Council Chamber on Thursdsy evening under the presidency of Alderman Pierpoint. There was a large attendance. The SECRETXRY read the minutes of the meeting of the sub-Committee held on Monday evening (Mr. Charles Plinston in the chair), which stated that the Secretary reported that he had written to the Secretary of the Co-operative Society with reference to the hiring of the Co-operative Hall. The Secretary of the Co-cperative Hail had written to say that he would bring the matter before the next meeting of the Society. After a prolonged and animated discussion, rules were drawu up and it was resolved that they be brought before the Society. Mr. W. CHRIMES said that the relolution passed at the last meeting was that the Society should be governed in accordance with the British House of Commons. The Society 1 included clergy and there was no clergy in the House of Commons. He did not, however,want the clergy excluded. The SEcREraRY rose to a point of order. THE RULES. _ . .

Considerable discussion- then took place on the draft of the rules submitted. The principal rules are : " The Society shall consist of members each subscrib- ing 28. 6d. per annum. honorary members ss. per annum, who should enjoy all the privileges of the Society. Members joining for he second half of the session is. Od. _ _ .

I The Warrington Parliamentary Debating Society is established for the purpose of discussing political and social topics, according, as far as may be practic- able, to the forms of the House of Commons. As in the House of Commons the House shall be divided into a Government (hereafter call the Ministry) with its supporters and an Opposition. The manage- ment of the general business of the Society shall be vested in a Council, consisting of the Speaker, the Chairman of Committees, the Treasurer, the Trustees, the Clerk of the House, the Honorary Secretary, and twelve elected members, all of whom with the excep- tion of the Trustees shall be elected annually. Five shall form a quorum. Any member of the Council , absenting himself without reasonable excuse consecu- tively from three of its ordinary meetings shall cease to be a member of the Council. Vacancies arising during the Session shall be filled up at the next ordinary meeting. The Ministry shall be entitled to claim pre- cedence for their measures. Other measures shall be taken according to their order it the minute book. The Ministry shall be composed of not fewer than eleven members. A list of such ministry, stating their official positions, shall be laid on the table of the House on the introduction of their first measure. The House shall meet every Thursday evening at 7.30 from the last Thursday in September to the Ist of December, and from the Ist of January to the Ist Thursday in April. The Speaker shall have power to suspend, for the sitting,any Member contravening the rules of the House, or failing to comply with the orders of the Speaker. Such Member's conduct shall be dealt with in 4 Com- mittee._ _ .

The House in Committee shall have power to expel from the Society an offending member by a vote of not less than three-fourths of those present and voting.

ELECTION OF OFFrCERS.

Nir. RANDLES moved that Alderman Bennett be elected Speaker of the House. Alderman BF 'NETT said he could not attend to the duties as he had such a great amount of engagements to fulfil. Mr. MAsoN said the success of the Society depended on the Speaker. (Hear, hear ~) He moved tbat the Council nominate a sub-coenfnittee who would report at the next meeting. Mr. F. G. TAYLOR seconded and the motion was carried.

Mr. MASON proposed " that Councillor Steel be appointed chairman of committees." It was necessary that a legal gentleman should fulfil that office. Mr. GARSIDE seconded and the motion was carried unanimously, Mr. T. S. SITEr in reply thanked the meeting and accepted the °Face, saying he would to the best of his ability give both sides fair judgment. Mr. OSBORNE moved that the Speaker and Chairman of committees be appointed trustees. Mr. BRADSHAW seconded and the motion was carried

„ Mr. P. H. Bennett was appointed trenaurer, Mr. E. Osborne secretary, and Mr. W. Mason, clerk of the House. Messrs. F. G. Taylor. W. H. Stansfield, G. Smith, C. Plinston,R.Tierce Williarns,M. G. Hearn, A. Kilburn, W. H. Sherburn, G. Reeves, E. Sogland, G. F. Awbery, E. Gordon Burgess were the twelve elected members of the Council.

PROTECTING THE CHILDREN. . The Work of the N.S.P C.C. in Warrington

The annual meeting of the Warrington branch of the National Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Children was held at the Town Hall, yesterday after- noon. At the commencement the Mayor presided and there was a good attendance., including the hon. secretary (Mr. W. D. Jeans, jun.). The MAXOI2 warmly commended the work of the Society in Warrington and paid a high tribute to the conscientiousness and tact of Inspector Cox. As his Worship had to leave to attend another meeting, the chair was then taken by Mr. C. E. Parker. THF, WORK OF THE YEAR—INFANTILE MORTAL-I-TY. _ _

In- his annual report the SECRETARY said the corn I mittee had pleasure in reporting a decrease in the I number of cases requiring investigation. Three hundred I and eighty-nine cases were dealt with, affecting 908 I children as against 331 in the preceding year. One thousand six hundred and twenty-six supervision visits were made during the year. An analysis of the cases showed that they practically all occurred in families where one or both of the parents were of drunken habits. Cases of actual brutality and violence were I very rare. The finances of the branch continued to be satisfactory, the balance of income over expenditure I being £lO 16s. 11d. The infantile death rate to which I reference was made in last year's report showed no sign of decreasing. A frequent cause of the death of young children was the wrong and foolish way in which I their mothers fed them. It might ultimately save many I lives if the elder children in elementary schools were given a course of instruction in the management and , feeding of young children. Sir GILBERT GREENALL was re-elected President and the Vice-Presidents and the Executive Committee were re-elected. The Council was re-elected with the addition of Mrs. Venn, Mrs. Dimelow, Mrs. Pitt, Mrs. McClure, Mr. IP. Awbery, Min Frost, Mr. J. Moore Murray, Misses Geddes and Mr. W. Roberts. WHAT VIE SOCIETY IS DOINa• An interestinc, address on the work of the Society was delivered by Mr. R. J. Parr, Director of the Society, who referred with satisfaction to the influ- ence which it was exerting on public opinion. The clay I would come when if would be impossible for any child to suffer in an enlightened community without someone being concerned about it. Although during the past year 44,433 cases of cruelty were reported, affecting 115,000 children, there was the comforting reflection that the Society had been able to help to that extent those who suffered and they were each year discovering more and more what was going on. Brutality was being gradually stamped out and it was becoming leas necessary every year to engage in prosecutions. (Applause.)

For Chronio Chest Complaint., Woods' Peppermint Care.—Advt.

WARRINGTON INFIRMARY.

New Medical Appointments.

LORD CROSS TO OPEN THE EXTENSION.

A special general meeting of the Governors of the Warrington Infirmary and Dispensary was held in the new pavilion at the Infirmary yesterday afternoon under the presidency of Sir Gilbert Greenall, Bart. There was a large and representative attendance, including the Mayor, Rev. H. V. Pigot (chairman of the Board of Management), Mr. W. P. Rylands (chairman of the Extension Committee), Dr. Fox (hon. consulting medical officer) and Mr. J. H. J. Hampson (secretary).

===== 0002601_19080118_art0071.txt | included=true | included =====
NE W VICE-PRESIDENT.

„ The Rev. Father WHITTLE moved that Mr. Henry Greensll be elected a Via-President of the institution and referred to the immense interest which Mr.Greenall took in the recent bazaar which realized between 0,000 and £3,000. Mr. C. JACKSON HOLMES seconded and the vote was heartily carried.

MEDICAL APPOINTMENTS. The Rev. Jorm YONGE moved : "That one of the honorary assistant medical officers be appointed a full honorary medical officer." He said he was sure they would all agree with him that they could not do a better thing for the Infirmary than to ask Dr. E. J. Fox to accept the appointment. He was fully qualified for it not only by his medical attainments but by his faithful services while he was honorary assistant medical officer. He had made record attendances and had been most anxious to assist the poor people who were in physical trouble. In making the appointment they would do credit to themselves and give great satis- faction to all who were associated with the insti ution. The Rev. C. HARVEY COOK seconded and the appoint- ment was unanimously agreed to. - Two applications—from Dr. G. Binns and Dr. A. liodgkinson—were re-eived for the post of honorary assistant medical officer. The Board of Management recommended the appointment of Dr. Hodgkinson. Mr. J. H. SMETHURST moved that Dr. Hodgkinson be appointed. He had done splendid work in connec- tion with the institution as senior house surgeon during the past five years, and had shown a particular interest in the extension scheme, his advice and skill having proved exceedingly valuable. Mr. W. P. RYLA SOS seconded. He had known Dr. Hodgkinson a considerable time, and the services he bad rendered to the institution were undeniable, and of the very highest value. The appointment was unanimously agreed to. THE RULES. CHURCHES AND THEIR SUBSCRIPTIONS. The next business was the amendment of rules, a long list of alterations being moved by the Rev. H. V. PIGOT, on behalf of the Board of Management. The principal change is that the Institution be henceforth governed by rules and bye-laws instead of by rules only ; that no bye-law shall contravene a rule, and that the rules be made by the Governors, and the bye-laws be made by the Board of Managetnent. That the bye- laws apply to the resident medical staff, matron, nursing staff, and servants. A new rule is that—" All donors of 500 gnineas or upwards, the same being a personal gift, and not the result of any collective effort, shall be Vice-Presidents for life." The Rev. J. F. Mkayrrlmvs called attention to a pro- posal that " The representative of any place of worship in which a collection of three guineas or upwards is made in aid of the funds of the Institution, shall be a Governor during one year from the date of payment." He said that under the old rule the amount of the collection was fixed at a mirnium of one guinea and the alteration would have the effect of disfranchising a number of small congregations. The Rev. H. V. PIGOT said the proposal was made by Mr. Thornton with the object of stimulating all the places of worship to a higher level of liberality. The Rev. J. F. MA:rrirEws : It seems to be apply- ing the lash to congregations. The CHAIRMAN : "Helping the lazy ones on with a stick." (Laughter.) The Rev. J. F. MATTHEws said small congregations had great difficulty in responding to the calls on behalf of various institutions. Mr. RYL ANDS said the chief idea of the Board was to alter the rule so that any representative of a con- gregation might be a governor, instead of confining it to the clergy or ministers. Ultimately it WM decided to revert to the original amount of one guinea as a minimum. THE EXTENSION. Mr. RYL ANDS announced that the Extension Com- mittee had been fortunate in securing a promise from Lord Cross to open the new buildings on March 19th. He would remind the meeting that they still required .£l,OOO and he hoped an effort would be made to raise I it before the opening day.

PALACE AND HIPPODROME.

"The Dandy Thieves."

Merriment holds sway at the Palace and Hippodrome this week and those who want to he thoroughly amused could not do better than visit this popular house. The name cf Fred Karno is synonymous with fun of the first water, and the second half of the programme is sustained by his capital company in the successful musical extravaganza entitled "The Dandy Thieves." There is not a dull moment from beginning to end and shrieks of laughter greet the adventures of the bold had burglars and the valiant police who deem it best to hide round the nearest corner when there is a prospect of coming to close quarters with the wrong-doers. The piece is presented in three scenes and several catchy musical numbers prove highly. popular, notably the famous "Six little burglars." Mr. George Hester, as Sergeant Lightning is the life and soul of the performance and his doings whilst in charge of Miffin's Emporium are irresistibly comical. Mr. Frank Morton, as Lord Fa.sem, alias Flash Jack, sings with fine spirit "I'm King of the Burglars," his robust voice being used to the best advantage. The programme includes some other features of more than usual interest. The Davenports, lightning magicians and illusionists, give one of the best conjuring entertainments seen in Warrington for-a long time. The audience is com• pletely mystified by a series of wonderful tricks, for in respanse to a wave of the magic wand, live doves, yards of handkerchiefs and flags appear apparently from nowhere. Yeager and Da Costa figure in smart American Coonisms, their dancing being particularly clever. Barry Hillier is an accomplished vocalist and instrumentalist and does some surprising things in the way of artistic paper-tearing. SORIA very pleasing songs. beautifnlly illustrated, are given by Fox and Melville, and W. Foster, comedian, se run a dis- tinct success in his up te-date turns. Raymond's Bin-Tableaux, always interesting, has this week some side-splitting pictures depicting the wild career of the "Reckless Horseman" Widclop and his orchestra do themselves great credit in the bright selections " Royal Standard" (Darezriski) and " Romance and Two Dances" (Cerman).

===== 0002601_19080118_art0073.txt | included=true | included =====
NEXT WEEK'S PROGRAMME.

Patrons of the Palace should make a point of seeinm next week's entertainment which will be brimful of interest and fun. The bill is headed by Albert and Edmund's great comedy troupe in their original farcical ' and pantomimical real life absurdity, entitled "Neighbours," which will be presented in two scenes, and will provide something to laugh at, think over and remember. Another excellent turn will be provided by Satanella Trio, sensational acrobatic dancers, and amongst other artistes who will contribute to a splendid programme, are: —Fox and Melville, with illustrated songs : Amos Howard, coon singer and dancer ; Mabel Mavis, the wonderful musician; Wake and Dean, the argumentative gentlemen ; Wilman, instrumentalist.

ROYAL COURT THEATRE.

" Leah Kleschna."

With such an excellent attraction as Messrs. Armitage and Leigh's company in "Leah Kleschna,' good houses have been the rule at the Royal Court Theatre this week. The author, C. M. S. McLellan, has gone quite off the beaten track to discover a promising theme, and he has succeeded in weaving a story of great power and sustained interest. Leah Kleschna is the daughter of a clever professional thief, and has herself been trained as an expert in the art of burglary. When the play opens she is about to undertake a robbery, carefully planned by her father, at the house of Paul Sylvaine, an eminent public man, who, it is known, has come into possession of jewels of fabulous value. It is clear that Leah has some qualms about the enterprise, but in response to Kleschna's none too gentle coaxing, she apparently banishes them and we see her in the early hours of the morning rifling Sylvaine's safe in a truly business- like way. She is in the act of annexing the jewels when Paul Sylvaine appears upon the scene and, in spite of her alarming threats, refuses to allow her to depart. Now Paul holds unique views mit criminoloa, believing that all persons, however degraded, have, some spark of goodness within them. and that they will prove more amenable to appeals to that better nature than to punishtnent. Therefore, instead of handing Leah over to the police, he talks to her in a gentle,persuasive way,which breaks down her proud spirit and arouses a lo ithing of her calling. Leah determines to break from the old life and loses little time in telling her father that she means to leave him. Of course, be is furious and storms and sleads in turn, but all to no avail. A highly dramatic and moving scene culminates in Leah goin, forth alone to begin life anew amid the haunts of her childhood in Austria. There she gains a livelihood by honest toil with the poor peasantry, until Paul Sylvaine seeks her out and makes her his wife. The company is one of the strongest we have seen in Warrington and the production merits the highest praise. Miss Bessie Riguold in the title role gives a thoroughly artistic and appealing ponrtrayal. Her acting is not of the hysterical order but has a quiet dignity and charm which is much more effective. She is particularly good in the trying encounter with Kleschna at the part- ing of the ways. Mr. Wm. F. Grant is an admirable Paul Sylvaine, displaying perfect composure under the most untoward circumstances and delivering the many fine lines of the part with due impressiveness. Mr. William Clayton gives a truly striking version of Kleschna and works himself up into a mighty passion when his designs are thwarted by Leah's reclamation. Mr. Gilbert Heron makes the most of the part of Schram whose devotion to Leah is pathetic. A special word of praise is due to Mr. Hamilton Deane who scores one of the greatest successes of the piece as the reckless, wayward Raone Berton. As the boister- ous reveller in Act 2 he does splendidly. NEXT WEEK'S PROGRAMME. Next week's attraction is one that should appeal powerfully to Warrington playgoers, as Mr. J. A. Campbell's principal company will appear in the new homely play "The Little Breadwinner." Mr. Camp- bell, who personally supervises the production of the. play, is the author of" The Sin of Her Childhood," "The Old Folks at Howe" and other highly popular dramas. " The Little Breadwinner" has met with cordial approbation throughout the land, and has been termed a worthy rival to "The Silver King." It is now in the thiol year of tour. Little Marjorie Lawrence will apnear in the role of " Meg," which she has performed with great success.

EDUCATION COMMITTEE.

Annual Increases of Teachers' Salaries.

The monthly meeting of the Education Committee will be held at the Town Hall on Monday evening when the businocs for consideration will include the following :

OFFICIALS' SALARIES. The Finance Committee recommend the following increases of salary from April Ist :—Mr. A. S. Elliot, stores clerk, from £143 10s. to £l5O ; Mr.l. C. Pharaoh, higher education clerk, from £94 to £lOO 10s. ; Mr. R. Price, statistical clerk, from £5B 10s. to £65 ; Miss M. E. Austin, typist, from £59 10s, to £l5 ; Mr. P. W. Smith. committee and minute clerk, from £39 to £45 10s.

TEACHERS' SALARIES. The School Management Committee submit the annual list of increases of salaries of elementary school teachers, the total amount of advances being £1,078. THE BOLTON SCHOOL. Letters were read from the Minister and Secretary of the Latchford Baptist Church as to the constitution of the body of Managers appointed for theinew Bolton Council school, and directions were given as to the reply to be sent thereto.

BATHING FACILITIES. A letter was read from the Town Clerk, intimating that owing to want of accommodation, the Town Hall, Parks and Baths Committee are unable to extend to school girls the facilities for attendance at the Baths afforded to school boys, but stating that the Committee have under consideration a scheme for the enlargement of the Baths under which the desired accommodation could be provided. The Director was instructed to state, in reply, that, whilst welcoming the announce- ment as to the intended provision of additional bathing accommodation, this Committee would be glad if the -Town Hall, Parks, and Baths Committee could see their way to make some arrangement by which school girls could, in the meantime, have the benefit of the existing bathing accommodation on say two days each week.

===== 0002601_19080118_art0077.txt | included=true | included =====
TRAINING COLLEGE ACCOMMODATION.

A. letter was read from the Lancashire County ' Connell, enquiring whether this Committee would be willing to co-operate with other Education Authorities in Lancashire in the provision of one or more Training Colleges for teachers, and the Director was instructed to intimate in reply, that, subject to a satisfactory scheme being prepared, this Committee would join with other Authorities for the purpose of providing additional Training College accommodation, and v. ould appoint representatives to meet representatives of the other Committees to dismiss the matter with them.

EMPLOYMENT OF SCHOOL t HILDHEN. The School Attendance Comm:ttee has considered the question of the terms of emnloyment of school children between the ages of 13 and 14, and resolved that a reply be sent to the communication received from the Warrington Head Teachers' Association upon this subject, to the effect that this Committee is not prepared to recommend the Education Committee to withdraw the discretionary power contained in the Bye-laws, and that they will continue to carry out the IBye-laws as they at present exist.

Wesleyan Home Missions. A meeting in support of Wesleyan Home Missions was held in Bold-street Wesleyan Church on Thursday evening. Mr. Jenks, of Penketh, presided over a fair attendance. The principal speaker was the Rev, T. Morcom Taylor, 8.A., of Bradford, who fcr thirteen years was engaged in mission work in Bradford. He dealt most interestingly and convincingly with work amongst the poor and outcast in the cities and large towns. The Rev. W. Mellor also took part in the zeting.

The Salvation Army's Immigration Work. Adjutant Hanna, of the Salvation Army Canadian Immigration, Toronto, delivered an interesting lecture on "Canada " in the Salvation Army Hall, Academy- street, on Monday evening. Some remarkable statistics with regard to the immigration work of the Army were first given by Mr. McCarthy, an Army officer from London, who explained that they had come to Warrington to give disinterested advice. During three-and-a-half years there had Failed to Canada under their auspices over 30,000 persons. From January to October last year 15,000 sailed, while they booked 719 persons to other parts of the world,. The Salvation Army had no free pmsages, as some people imagined. They were shipping and booking agents, and were making a decent business out of it and the more profits they made, the better they liked it. They charged the same rates as ordinary shipping ' agents and got the same profits, but those profits, instead of going to private individuals went towards assisting, by way of loan, needy persons who went to Canada. By that means the Army last year advanced £10,592, and £2,512 bad been repaid. As the money was repaid they advanced it to other suitable persons. At the present time their funds were low, and they had on their hands between 300 and 400 suitable families, whom they were pledged to assist to the extent of £7,850 as the money came in. Of the 30,000 persons they had conveyed to Canada, not more than 100 were out of employment at the present time. The Salvation Army had been asked by the Government in British Columbia to send out 1,000 men and women, for whom work was guaranteed, and the first party would sail on February 20th. The fare from Liverpool, London or Glasgow, to Vancouver, including a week's railway journey and board,was £l6. They had also been asked to send a party of female machinists and 200 domestfc servants to Canada, and good wages were guaranteed.—Adjutant Hanna, who has spent all his life in Canada, then gave an interest- ing account of his experiences, and spoke of the needs of that country. His observations were illustrated by limelight views.

LONGER AGO THAN YESTERDAY.

Yesterday is not long ago, but nearly three and a ball years is. To have been cured long ago and to have been well ever since, surely means.a permanent cure. Here is what a Newton-le-Willows woman has to say about it. Mrs. E. Cooke, -13, Golborne Dale-road, Newton-lb- Willows, writes :—" Dear sirs : For some time I was subject to acute pains in my back and stomach, and although I tried different treatments, I could get no relief. _ . _

"One day I happened to read about Doan's pills' and I sent for some to try. I have been a great deal better since using this rqedicine, and my back has been much easier. If the pains return, I shall take some more of Doan's pills. (Signed) Mrs. Cooke." Speaking to one of our representatives, threc and a half years later, Mrs. Cooke said :—" 1 still keep quite well. I was very bad when I first used Doan's pills. I had been fifteen weeks under medical treatment. I could not straighten my back, it was so bad, and I had severe heidaches and nasty giddiness also ; I had palpitation of the heart as well. I often think I would not have got better had it not been for Doan's pills, for although I took other medicines, I was growing weaker all the time. I am indeed grateful for my cure." If you vra suffering from Backache, Urinary Dis- orders, Rheumatism, Dropsy, Gravel, Neuralgia, or any other kidney trouble, Doan's Pills will cure you ; they will cure your kidneys, the cause of your illness. SCIENCE NOTES AND NEWS. WOMEN'S TEMPERANCE ASSCCIATION.

MICROBES AND COINS.

As the result of investigations, a Paris scientist has discovered that coins form a happy hunting ground for microbes, the most common species of which are staphylo- coccus, streptococcus, and pyogenes, with now and then a colony of tetanus, tubercu- losis, or cancer microbes. Gold pieces har- bour from 1,600 to 3,500 microbes, silver coins from 450 to 2,100, and bronze and nickel coins from 3,600 to 11,000.

===== 0002601_19080118_art0084.txt | included=true | included =====
MOSQUITOES . AND COLOURS.

Mosquitoes are found to prefer negroes to whites, a black dog to a white one, and a dark-coloured resting-place. Careful tests have been extended to great numbers of the insects, shewing that they choose colours in the order of dark blue, dark red, brown, red, black, grey .and violet, and that azure, ochre, and white are distasteful, and yellow ex- tremely so. Confirming these results on 150 mosquitoes a Swiss `malaria expert has found that three fourths eettle on dark colours.

===== 0002601_19080118_art0085.txt | included=true | included =====
POISON IN POTATOES.

Potatoes, under certain conditions, contain an alkaloidal poison known as solanin. Very little pf this is found in new potatoes, unless they have grown on the top of the ground, and the skin has become green. •It has gene- rally been known that such potatoes were poisonous, but the cause was unknown. Pota- toes that are kept over until late in the sum- mer also contain a large amount of this poison. It is said that in 1892 and 1893 a great many 'of the troops in the German Army were poisoned by the use of such pota- toes. Meyer found that old potatoes which were kept in a damp place and had begun to sprout contained twenty-four times as much of the solanin alkaloid as new potatoes.

===== 0002601_19080118_art0086.txt | included=true | included =====
A METEORIC MYSTERY.

Among the minerals known to chemists there are a few rare earths, containing ele- ments bearing such curious names as yttrium, ytterbium, erbium, aid scandium, which are not merely very scarce, but have .been found only in the northern hemisphere, chiefly in parts of Norway, Siberia, and the United States. Upon this very interesting fact, Sir William Crookes based a singular conclusion. He suggested that perhaps it might be that these elements luvve been formed not on our globe at all, but in some other portion of space, and may then have reached us in the forta of a shower, or showers, of meteoric stones. The supposed meteoric shower that brought them, falling at intervals during twenty-four hours on. the upper half of the northern hemisphere, would pass over Siberia, Norway, the North Atlantic, and North America. It is interest- ing to note that the discovery of a few years ago of terrestrial helium was made in one of these very rare earths, found in Norway, and called cleveite.

===== 0002601_19080118_art0087.txt | included=true | included =====
NEW OXIDE OF SILICON.

It has been generally held by chemists that silicon forms only one oxide, the familiar silica, which in the shape of flint, quartz, and sand forms such a considerable part of the earth's crust. The general re- semblance of the element silicon to carbon, however, suggests that, like the latter, it should have two corresponding to carbon monoxide and carbon dioxide. Silica is the dioxide, and Clemens Winkler, after trying in vain to prepare the latter, concluded that silicon did not form such an oxide. In the course of three papers which he read before the American Electro - Chemical Society, however, Dr. H. Potter seems, says the Globe, to have proved that a monoxide of silicon can be prepared.

===== 0002601_19080118_art0088.txt | included=true | included =====
iIOW PREPARED.

- Dr. Potter attributes Winkler's failure to obtain silicon monoxide to the compara- tively low temperature, I,ooodeg. C., at which he worked. Heating together silica and silicon in an electric furnace, Dr. Potter found that at from I,7oodeg. to I,Boodeg. C. a very brisk reaction occurs, with the pro- duction of a brown smoke. If the operation be carried out in an inert atmosphere this can be collected as a soft, brown, very fine powder. Physical and chemical analysis have convinced Dr. Potter that this is the monoxide of silicon. He proposes .to name the product of the reaction " monox," whether pure monoxide, or a mixture of this with silica and silicon. USES OF MONOX.

It is found that when negatively charged the fine particles of monox fly to cotton fibres, &c., and so form a screen which will filter out solid matter in the air. Thus, air might be sterilised by drawing it through such a sereen. It is further claimed that monox would make a valuable pigment in certain oil paints, especially for brickwork and for protecting iron.

===== 0002601_19080118_art0089.txt | included=true | included =====
A NOVE,.. EXPERIMENT.

Cultivators whose hind is composed to any extent of the common •clays may assure them- selves that they are also owners of unknown quantities of both alum and aluminium; and, further, writes Mr. James Scott in the Agricultural Economist, that although these prodttcts are procurable from the trouble- some stuff, the requisite processes involved in their extraction have hitherto proved of so costly a character as to preclude the ad- visability of such manufactures becoming at all widely favoqred among others besides the professional men. The beautiful metal aluminium is ob- tained by the artificial decomposition of pure clay. Naturally, the matter_ Is too technical for protracted reference in a slight sketch. Alum, too, as can be gathered from the sound of its name, is intimately associated with the metal, and has a similar origin. It would not be a difficult matter for those readers who burn their clay soil to secure supplies of this chemical if ' they cared to undertake the trouble. While clay is being chnsumed at a temperature causing it to as- sume a dull red hue, if it should then be properly acted upon by sulphuric acid— another substance used by all growers, and found combined in their various sulphates,— it will yield the every-day and useful alum. It was due to these reflections that I felt tempted to set forth the peculiar experi- ments' notified, believing that it would attract a little enthusiasm in some quarters.

===== 0002601_19080118_art0090.txt | included=true | included =====
BEAUTIFUL CRYSTALS.

Select a few crisp cinders of any conveni- ent , size from the fire-grate, and free them as far as possible from any ash and, other refuse. Then boil half a pint—or more—of water, and into it gradually drop some pow- dered alum, stirring it meantime with a glass rod. Continue dissolving the alum in this manner until the water will contain no more of it. Afterwards pour the compound into a cup or a glass, and suspend two or three of the, prepared cinders within it by tying a thread round each of them and over a penholder,. which wilt serve as a bridge across the top of the vessel holding the ob- jects. Allow all to stand undisturbed in some cool corner for a, day or two. When the cinders are withdrawn a charming, in- structive display will reward the experi- menter, as each otherwise paltry bit of waste will be covered with- beautiful sym- metrical crystals, resembling four-sided pyramidal diamonds of la'rge size. If these false "mineral spars" be kept away from contact with dirt for some time they will eventually become dense china-white in tint, while still retaining their very sharply- defined formation. Really magnificent effects mar be derived by staining the alum solu- tions with logvvood, some of the aniline compounds, or other dyes. Even red ink can he employed. You can deceive your friends, ;1 so minded, by making the crystals lard. _:niitly red, blue, green, violet, and so on. By dipping the cinders first in a red solution, and then scraping off some of the crystals

Childrea acd Public-Houses.

The Masonic Hall, Bold-street. was the scene of a public meeting on Tuesday evening under the auspices : of the British Women's Temperance Association. Mrs. ' Owen, of Penketh, presided, and was supported by the Mayoress and Mrs. Randolph Clarkson, of Birmingham, who gave en interesting address on the temperance question. Mrs. OvvEN, who is the President of the Association' in her openi rearl,s said she Was disappointed at ng cu the small attendance, but very often the greatest results came from small meetings. It was from the one or two j who made up their minds to do their best for those by whom they were surrounded that the greatest results came. She gave the audience as a motto fur the year the words' To-day," in connection with which she gave • some beautiful advice. She then called upon Mrs. Clarkson to give her address. I Mrs. CLARKSON said they wanted the public to show ! • their enthusiat.m in fighting tho drink evil. If they i went to the public-house they would find women with I their babies in their arms drinking away and spending their money in drink instcad of getting food for the , child. If the child uttered a cry it would get a sip of ' beer or gin r whatever the mother vas drinking. The speaker then went on to say that they were expecting • great things front Parliament.. A Bill has been promised I them, and they hoped it would Le a good one. (Hear, hear.) In every school temperance and hygiene should be tauebt Alcphol had been correctly deecrth.3a ag 'a mocker." It was not only a Meeker but deceptive and people were in the habit of taking it when cold under I the impression that it would warm the system. But that ' was a mistake, as there was more warmth in a slice of bread and a cup of cocoa. Alcohol reduced the tempera- • ture,and a man who took it to warm himself was colder In the end. It was only one in ten who was a teetotaler, but if all British women possessed the necessary. grit, faith and enthusiasm teetotalers would soon be in the majority, (Hear, hear and applause.) Several instances I were given of children who had died from neglect j owing to the drunkenness of their parents,and they had a right to ask that children should be safeguarded. They ought, as English men and English women, to be in advance of other countries in point of civilization, and ehe asked the people of Warrington to sign a petition which was to be got up for presentation to Parliament asking that no child should be allowed on licensed premises, and no one under eighteen supplied with intoxicating liquor. (Applause.) Their Association was strongly in favour of Local Veto. Everybody should be ccnsulted as to their wish for certain things. If some brewery wanted to build a public-house in a certain town that brewery should first consult the residents of that town. She believed that everybody had the right to be able to express an opinion in the government of the town they belonged to. (Hear, hear.) Temperance should be taught in Sunday Schools and then the rising generation would be a sober one. She never knew of anybody who signed the pledge to regret it. She as once told that a certain women lived on intoxicating liquors and could not,even if she tried, keep away from it. If that woman had sat end thought of the harm drink had done to her she could at once leave it alone. (Applause.) Return- ing to Local Veto she said they could only get it if they showed their influence in the flatter. A man who paid a rent of £SCO a year could pleare himself what was done round his neighbourhood, but a man who paid ss. a week rent could not. That ought not to be. Everyone had a right to express an opinion especially on the liquor traffic. (Applause.) Miss Cox theh gave two solos which reflected great credit upon her.

The tIRESIDENT also spoke on the importance of temperance teaching. She never regretted signing the pledge. the signed the pledge 53 years ago. (Applause.) Mrs. ROBERT GARNETT, in proposing a vote of thanks to Mrs. Clarkson for her convincing address, pointed out that Mr. G. P.. Sims' revelations with respect to alcohol seemed to he confirn.,A in every town, by Mrs. Clarkson so far as Birmingham was concerned, and by Mr. Sims to far aa London was concerned. Intemper- ance was dreadful in Warrington—at least they were told so—and in every toun, and yet Mrs. Clarkson was hopeful. Public opipion, about which they talked so much, was growing on the side of temperance. (Applause.) Customs were changing and the habits of the people also. Although the drink bill N‘its still a very heavy one they knew bow much less alcohol was to be seen ou the tables now at parties and entertainments. People were beginning to flnd out that they could do without drink, end the King had taught tht.m that they could drink his health quite as well in t• ater, and they thanked him for it. (Hear, hear and .-pplause.) Miss Ittssii DUTTON, in seconding, expressed the opinion that there was a change'for the better so far as the drinking habits of the people were concerned. There Nies hope to be deriv( cl from what was taking place. Things were ire/moving and that was pleasing,and a meeting like the present, altbotigh it was nut a large one, helped things to move a little quicker. (Hear,hear, and applause.) The motion was carried unanimously and a similar compliment to Mrs. Owen was also paid, the meeting concluding with a hymm.

UTTERS TO THE EDITOR.

NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS. Authors of letters intended for publication must in all cases send their names and addresses to the Editor as a guarantee of good faith. Matter intended for publication must be written on one side only of the paper and sent in, if possible, not later than Thursday. Is Interest Robbery?

_ Sir,—l cannot refrain from again replying to Mr. Parker, after the charge he makes against the Socialists In last week's letter, namely," that he has good reason to complain of the action of the Socialists, who fre- quently make random charges, and when asked to make them good run away. They preach a creed which they , are afraid, when challenged, to put into practice, a creed for show and not for use," and refers me to John Workman's last letter. I read the last reply of John Workman and I read Mr. Parker's challenge, which I thought was one of the most ridiculous proposals that ever emanated from the mind of any person who professed a knowledge of economics. Socialists do not propose to experiment with isolated colonies consisting of self 20 Men to find out the practicability of Socialism.Somalists know that the principles of Mr. Parker's challenge if experimented with would be doomed to failure immediately, and why? Because the wages to be pooled would have to be earned under the competitive system which would not guarantee to each one of them a regular wage, neither would it guarantee that each One would be regularly employed, or prevent the cost of commodities rising and thus lowering each man's purchasing power. And last but not least important, what employer is going to give them the full reward of their labour, viz., the missing two-thirds (see Chiozza Money,"Riches and Poverty") ? I think John Workman was wise in not allowing him- self to be trapped. In reference to our creed being for show and not for use I admit it is a show and I claim there is no other show on earth like it. Anyone who steps inside will be shown gratis all the facts and figures relating to human struggles to live and I think we are just a wee bit useful. I notice 333,000 votes were given in 1006 to 30 Labour men now in the House of Commons (see Press reports of their usefulness) and again I think when the people awake from their apathy and learn tae true and only cause of their misery our creeds will prove their usefulness. And now to Mr. Parker's other points in respect to the horse and cart problem. I have nothing to add to my former answer. That is to say if the horse and cart decreased in value the user should make them good. Mr. Parker wanted an answer from an ethical point of view and I understand ethics to be a system of moral principles ; that being so there is no moral law applied to industry where a man has to depend on another man for the implements of production before he can make nature yield unto him that by which he shall live. And again what use does the owner sacrifice, suppose he makes use of his horse and cart for himself ? He would have to labour, so he lends his horse and cart and sacrifices his privilege to labour. I understand the meaning of the word sacrifice to be something sacred, a religious act (see Latin roots). Would the owner of the horse and cart be paying homage to his Maker by sacrificing his labour ? see Paul's epistle, " He that toileth not neither shall he eat." So much for thehorse and cart problem. Now sir, Mr. Parker says, "arising out of his machine and seamstress problem," I have ascribed a word to him he never used. Well it did not need an inch and half of space to tell me so and Mr. Parker used words that gave me the impression he meant inventor. Anyway I accept the correction, the word maker will do for me, and I again say the maker should be paid for his labour, but don't think the seamstress gets the 3s. she saves in the cost of production by using the machine, neither is the real value of the articles equal. They are only equal in value from a utility point of view, but in exchange value they are not equal because one cost 12s. and the other 9s. to make, and the latter form of value determines the market price. But the point I want to deal with is Mr. Parker's claim that capital is the friend and helper of labour and his typical instance of the machine saving the seamstress 3s. I stated in my last letter that labour was not assisted by capital as a friend, neither was a living wage guaranteed, yet the production by the use of the machine was greater. I can best prove this,or at any i rate try to con- vince Mr. Parker, that the seamstress s not the gainer .of 35., by giving a case that came under my notice. Some time ago I visited the Sweated Industries Exhi- bition in Manchester, and there I saw women making shirts, from Bd. up to Is. 7d. per doze,' shirts, buying their own thread and needles and paying Is. per week for hire of machine. One of these women told me she used to receive a shilling for making one shirt by baud for the high-class trade, and could earn good wages ; now she earns from 6s. to 7s. per week for making about seven dozen shirts. This woman makes shirts for the shipping trade, and she can either take the material home to make up or work at the shirt factory. The above statement is not an isolated case but one of the thousands under our present sytem of industry. I think the net gain after paying for hire of the machine in the above and other cases like it goes to the capitalistic shirt manufacturer and not the seamstress. And now, in conclusion, may I say that Victor Gray- son never said anything in his speech to lead people to understand the Socialists were going to rob labour or the capitalist. What he did say was—We did not want the highly questionable elements of rent, interest and profits, and that he believed wages should be the funda- mental result of work. Mr. Parker says if I can show him where he is wrong he is willing and ready to acknowledge it, and expects me as an honest man to do the same, if he proves that lam wrong. Well, Mr. Parker has not said anything yet to convince me that money makes money without the aid of labour, and if the man with £lOO can make it Into £l5O without an effort mentally or physically he is either a conjuror or a robber? I do not doubt Mr. Parker's honesty, nor do I begrudge him the interest he draws, but I cannot fall in with his views of capital and interest, neither can I honestly support the system Mr. Parker agrees with.—Yours very truly, GEO. REEVES.

Stockton Heath, January 15th, 1908.

[AnvT.] THE WARRINGTON EA.. COOPERATIVE

SOCIETY LTD., AND MR. JOHN TAYLOR, L.D.S. The Committee of the Warrington Co-operative Society have had their attention drawn by Mr. Taylor of Museum-street, to the fact that a notice continued in the last balance sheet of the Society issued to its members, referring to "Dental Services " is considered a breach of the arrangements at to advertising entered into by Mr. Taylor with the Dental Association. The Committee regret that owing to a misunder- standing as to the nature of the notice that any difficulty has been caused and wish to state that steps have been taken to withdraw as far as possible the notice from circulation and at the same time at Mr. Taylor's request the arrangements entered into as far as he is concerned have been cancelled. Signed on behalf of the Committee. J. JARMAN, Secretary.

WARRINGTON MUSICAL FESTIVAL.

Latest Arrangements

The arrangements for the first Warrington Musical I Festival, which has been definitely fixed for May 23rd, are being rapidly advanced. The Mayor (Alderman Stnetburst) has acceded to the cordially-expressed wish that he should act as President and it is hoped that the festival will receive the support of all lovers of music in Warrington. A thoroughly representative committee has the project in hand and frequent meet- ings are held. The selection of Dr. W. G. McNaught and Mr. Wilfred Jones as adjudicators should give general satisfaction, as both these gentlemen have had a wide experience of festival work. NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

RUGBY FOOTBALL.

By " Ovalist."

England has won her first real international under professional Rugby rules, and Engl-ind was lucky to do so. The New Zealanders were far and away the superior team in the great match at Wigan on Satur- day, and were distinctly unfortunate to lose by the margin of a goal after scoring sixteen points. What must have made the defeat more galling was that Messenger, the most expert of goal-kickers, failed to land a goal from a penalty shut right in front of the posts, while two tries were certainly missed through a faulty pass almost on the line. The English side was a strong one in all respects and very dangerous near the line, but three out of the four tries credited to the Old Country were lucky. All the New Zealand points came as the result of superlatively skilful foot- ball, and there was no luck about any of them.

The stay-at-homes on Saturday must have beea feeling "pretty sick " since Saturday, for nearly all the papers unite in praising the international game and the display of the All Blacks. The match was certainly the finest I ever saw, not only for its sustained interest and thrilling incidents, but for the quality of the football shown. The English team played a good, orthodox game,and showed some smart passing at times, but as a side they were outclassed by their opponents. The All Blacks played the game as the original team played it at the expense of the Rugby Unionists. They certainly transcended any display given on the present tour and left no doubt as to their exceeding cleverness. Whether they suddenly found their best form for the first time I do not know, but the passing by the five-eighths and threequarters on Saturday was positively electrifying. Smith, Todd, and Messenger were suberb in their pace and combination, and the English backs were fre- quently baffled completely and left standing.

The contrast in styles between English and New Zealand football was most marked. England's passing from halves to three-quarters was quick and well-timed, but it was too stereotyped. On the other hand you never knew which way the ball was going when the All Blacks were on the move. It was no sooner sent to one wing than it WOAt back t centre again. It was pass and re-pass all the time, and the way the inside men went through the English defence was startling. With England's luck, and strength 'forward, the All Blacks might easily have run up 30 points. They were worth more than they got, but in any case it was a magnificent performance to score 2 goals, 4 tries, after being 8 points behind in the first ten minutes.

At half back or five-eighths —which is practically the same thing—Todd Was superior to anybody on the English side, and I doubt if we have as fine a player in this position in the country. Smith and Messenger were too fast for the home harks, and worked together in beautiful style. R. Wynyard too, gave a great display at the base of the scrummage and scored two tries. The All. Blacks were a bit unsteady in defence, and the forwards had to play second-fiddle to the English vanguard both in scrummage and foot work. But here England's advantage ceased. Taylor (full back), Leytham and Eccles were alone comparable to the men opposed to them. The halves played a smart game individually, but neither Lomas nor Hogg was first-class at centre, and showed little resource. There ought to be a few changes for the test games.

To-day the All Blacks play the chosen of Lancashire at Oldham, and given a dry turf they will have an excellent prospect of winning, especially as the men have had a full week's rest. The Lancashire team is, in my opinion, quite as strong as the one which repre- sented England and is as follows : Back : H. Gifford (Barrow). Three-quarters : J. Leythem (Wigan), left wing ; B. Jenkins (Wigan), left centre ; A. Hogg (Broughton Rangers) (capt.), right centre ; G. Tyson (Oldham), right wing. Half-backs : J. Baxter (Rochdale), J. Jolley (Runcorn). • Forwards : J. Padbury (Runcorn), A. Smith (Oldham). J. Beetham (Broughton Rangers), F. Ruddick (Broughton Rangers), F. H. Shugars (War- rington), and A. Buckler (Salford). . Reserves.-- J. Sharrock (Wigan), full-back; G. Dickenson (Warrington), centre three-quarter ; C. Creevey (Wigan), wing three-quarter; W. D. John (Salford). half-back ; B. Avery (Oldham), J. Spencer (Salford), and W. Cheetham (Wigan), forwards.

The All Blacks' record up-O-date is: played 23, won 15, lost 11, drawn 2, with aggregate score of 331 points to 232. Further matches are being arranged well into March, and the clubs which will probably get a second match are Warrington, Wigan, Halifax, Oldham, Broughton, and St. Helens. While history was being made at Wigan,the Warring- ton men were distinguishing themselves at Barrow. Their victory by 21 points to 9 came as a pleasant sur- prise, for with the weak team which had to do duty an adverse result was feared by the majority of supporters. Barrow had only lost one match on their own ground prior to Saturday, but they had to play second fiddle to the Warringtonians, who were in their best form. The forwards paved the way by making good use of their superior physique, and the backs gave a sparkling display.

Lloyd worked the scrummages well and combined very effectively with Brooks. Another gratifying feature was the return to form of Isherwood. He and Fish performed in the style which rendered them famous as a left-wing pair, and between them they got three of the tries. Fish also showed more pace, and won a great race with Gifford, the County full back, from half-way. The victory has clone Warrington good in a statistical sense, and they now hold a fair position in the League table. They should yet sur- pass Hull and Salford in the race, but are rather along way removed from the first four.

The League table up-to-date is as follows : For. Agst. Per- P. W. L. D. G. T. P. G. T. P. c't'ge. The Examiner, Saturday, January 18, 1908.

THE LAWSON CONTRACT.

AWARD IN FAVOUR OF CORPORATION.

Contractor Has to Pay £1432.

Much satisfaction has been felt in Warrington during the past week at the result of the arbitration proceedings in connection with the famous Lawson contract. The award of the arbitrator (Ur. George Henry Hill, M.1.C.E., of Manchester and Westminster) was officially made known at the meeting of the Water Committee on Monday afternoon and, briefly stated, its purport is that the contractor (Mr. Gorge Lawson, of Rutherglen, Lanark, Scotland) shall pay £1,432 to the Corporation and a considerable sum in costs.

To recall the history of the case, it will b 3 remem- bered that as long ago as the 4th December, 1905, the Water Committee proposed to appoint, or consented to the appointment of Mr. George Hill as arbitrator, but the Corporation then refused to agree to the appointment. Subsequently a Lawson Investigation Committee was formed to go into the matter and report to the Council. In the meantime the plaintiff issued a writ against the Corporation in which ha claimed the sum of £36,425 is. as the balance due to him under the contract, which was signed on the 21st September, 1900. The contract price was £24,400, but there were other payments to be paid to the contractor largely dependent on the contingencies. The Corporation had paid to the contractor the sum of £35,481, and this, with the am mat of his claim endorsed on the writ, made a total claim of £71,909 is. When the writ was issued the Corporation took ont a summons to dismiss the action on the ground that the plaintiff PrLl4 bOUIId to go to arbitration under the terms of the contract, and the Court, with the consent lof both parties, made an order referring all matters lin dispute to Mr. Hill as sole arbitrator. The Cor- poration then sent in counter-claims against the con- tractor, principally for delay in carrying out their work, and costs incuntd in consequence, amounting in the aggregate to about £8,693 133. 11d., including £3,093 3s. 6d. for pumping plant put down in 1902 and £3,777 93. id. claimed on interest in unremunera- tive capital expenditure. The arbitration proceedings, which occupied eleven days, commenced on the 14th October, at the Surveyor's Institute, London. The counsel for the plaintiff (Mr. George Lawson,c )ntractor, of Rutherglen Lanark, Scotland), were Mr. Radcliffe, K.C., and Mr. G. A. Scott (instructed by Messrs. Gordon, Faulkner land Fairweather, of Edinburgh), and Mr. A. A. Hudson and Mr. Arnold Inman (instructed by the Town Clerk of Warrington), for the Corporation. Mr. Hill made his award on the 6th inst. but it was not taken up until Saturday, The following is an extract from the award : —" I do award and deter- mine that the Corporation is not indebted to the contractor in any sum, and I further award and determine that the contractor is indebted to the Corporation in the stun of £1,432, and I direct that the, contractor do forthwith pay the said sum of £1,432 to the Corporation, and I further , award and direct that the Corporation do bear their own costs of the action, and do pay to the contractor his costs of the action, such costs being set off against the costs hereby directed to be paid by the contractor to the Corporation ; and I further award and direct that the contractor do bear and pay his own costs of the reference, and do pay to the Corporation their costs of and incident to the reference. And I settle and fix the costs of this my award at the sum of £382 103., and if the Corporation pay the same I direct that the contractor do repay to the Corporation the amount so paid." Briefly, the result of the award is that the con- tractor has to repay to the Corporation the sum of £1,432. Whether this is in respect of moneys over- paid or of the Corporation's counter-claim, is not stated. The contractor has also to pay all the costs of the arbitration, including the costs of the arbi- trator's award. On the other hand the Corporation have to pay the costs of the action, which, we under- stand, will be small, because nothing was done a fter the issue of the writ but the taking out of a summons to have the action referred to arbitratioa. The Corporation are entitled to deduct the costs of the action from the costs of the arbitration payable by the contractor.

At the ordinary meeting of the Water Committee, held on 'Monday afternoon, Alderman Evans presiding, a resolution expressing entire satisfaction with the way the action had been cmducted on behalf of the Corporation was unanimously passed.

===== 0002601_19080118_art0105.txt | included=true | included =====
PARR HALL. -- Mr. J. W. Turners English Opera Co.

The week devoted to English opera at the Parr Hall has made a pleasant break in the round of melodrama and vaudeville entertainments to which local theatre goers have grown accustomed. Although the attendance at Parr Hall was not large during the early part of the week, Mr. Turner's productions have grown in popularity with the result that Thursday brought a fairly full house to witness the ever-welcome" Faust." And without at all disparaging the performances on other nights it will probably be admitted that Thursday's performance marked a culminating point in the opera company's efforts, for principals chorus and orchesta alike contributed to a flue presentment of Gounod's masterpiece during which the audience's enthusiasm showed itself in numerous encores and reoalls. Of the two operas "It Trovatore " and the "The Lily of Killarney," in which Mr. Turner appeared in a leading part, it may be said unhesitatingly that the march of time devoted to many years' of work leaves that veteran better prepared to portray the part of " Miles - na-Coppaleen "than that of" Maurico." And although this arises from a purely physical defection it is but due to say that Mr. Turner's voice remains singularly clear and sweet, and free from the ravages which time alone might have been expected to make upon it and upon his ability to produce silver-like notes of high range with little effort. Mr. Turner has surrounded himself with a strong company who are,in all essentials, both competent and capable. The cast and the chorus in" Faust" demonstrated that the opera company's resources were at least adequate to deal with the exigencies of the composer's score, though it cannot be denied that the mounting of the work equally revealed the weakness attending all travelling companies and also the poverty of scenic material available along with the Parr Hall proscenium. The " prima donna " has been personated during the week by the three ladies Miss Sarah Williams, Miss Kitty Brownlees and Miss Christine Gomez who have all distinguished themselves brilliantly and more or less ingratiated themselves with the audiences, while the leading tenor and bass parts have been in the hands of Mr. Turner, Mr. Charles le Suer, Mr. Tom Griffiths, and Mr. Sidney Clifford, all of whom have also made themselves favourites in coarse of the week. The chorus deserve a word of commendation for the whole-hearted way in which they sang the" Faust " choruses and for their general all-round efficiency. The orchestra was largely corn- posed of local instrumentalists, and the necessity of ' their having to play new or strange works each evening did not contribute to either smooth productions or artistic execution of the band parts. Nevertheless, under the skilful guidance of the Opera Co.'s hard- working conductor Mr. T. Lawton,who himself rendered invaluable help at the piano, matters were here fairly satisfactory, and occasionally in some of the bigger ensembles the playing was distinctly fine. At the concluding performance this evening" Maritana " is pretty sure to draw a big house.

PADGATE.

NEW STATIONMASTER.-11r. Edward Roberts, of Otterspool Station, C.L.C., has been appointed to succeed Mr. A. E. Roberts, stationmaster of Padgate Station, who has been transferred to St. James' Station, Liverpool. The exchange will be made in about a fortnight's time.

EXTRAORDINARY COTTON PROFITS.

It was reported on Change on Wednesday, at Man- chester, that the Howe Bridge Spinning Company,which produces fine yarns made from Egyptian and Sea Island cotton, has made a profit during the last six months equal to 60 percent. per annum, but is only paying 20 per cent, per annum.

ST. AUSTIN'S BYE-ELECTION

KEEN CONTEST.

An exceptionally keen contest is in progress for the representation of St. Austin's Ward rendered vacant by the elevation of Councillor Burton to an aldermanic seat. Mr. John Maginn has come forward as an Independent and is being opposed by Mr. James Broadhurst (C) The candidates and their workers have entered into the struggle with great vigour and meetings have been held on both sides. The election will take place on Monday next and there will be two polling stations—at Thewlis-street school and Cairo- street school.

Mr. Magian' address will be found on our front page.

===== 0002601_19080118_art0109.txt | included=true | included =====

Mr. MAGINN described the question as " ridiculous." Did Mr. Randles think he had nothing else to do but to get up statistics with regard to air space in the various Schools ? He then stated that 21s. was too small a wage for a married man. He believed in paying a man what he ' was worth, but he wanted to know where such a motion would stop. On the other hand those who voted against the motion were Aldermen Tinnion and Barton, Councillors Wilkinson, Hulse, Sankey, Fulton-Smith, and the Mayor. Meeting in Tnewlis-street School

An enthusiastic meeting in support of the candidature of Nlr. John Magian was held in the Workingmen's Mission Schoolroom, Thewlis-street, on Tuesday even- ing. There was a large attendance, presided over by Alderman Tinnion. Amongst those present were the Rev.C. Harvey Cook, Mr.S. Wright and iAIr.E. England.

VOTE OF CONFIDENCE.

Mr. E. ENGLAND moved a resolution declaring Mr. Maginn to be a fit and proper person to represent the Ward. The Conservative party, he said, had thrown a great deal of dust in the eyes of the electorate over the Education question. He believed that the question should not be introduced in municipal contests, and that the Conservatives were playing the game very low in dragging Imperial politics into Council elections. (Applause.) Mr. S. WRIGHT, in seoonding, said he had always found Mr. Maginn to be independent. He could understand a working man becoming a Socialist, but be could not understand one becoming a Conservative. They wanted representatives who had minds of their OWD. M

night. He maintained that his brother and he paid more rates in the ward than the whole array of wealth on the Conservative platform. Workingmen, he con- tended, would be ashamed of the attempt made to foist ridiculous-doctrines upon them by swells coming in motor cars. They were told that if they did not elect Mr. Broadhurst, they would lose the Empire and St' Austin's Ward would slide into the river. (Laughter.) Why was this great array of power brought against a poor furniture dealer who once wheeled a barrow for 14s. a week in Warrington? What had he done to this crowd? Were they frightened of an honest man getting a public position, or was it that they were afraid he would rob them of their breweries, estates and motor cars? Did they mean to say this array of wealth was brought against him in the interest of their dear friend Mr. Broadhurst ? (Cries of" No.") Referring to the needs of the ward, Mr.Maginn emphasised the necessity for a better approach to the borough. He also asked if the aristocratic portion of the ward would tolerate Sankey Brook? (" No.") Yet the workers at the Sankey Bridges end had to live near it and some of their brothers and sisters bad died owing to that wretched,darnnable brook. The Corporation could not stop the stream but it would not take very much to cover the brook at least within the area where da oiling-houses were to be found. Then they must consider their friends the brewers, for he always loved to have a dig at them. (Laughter.) He had no objection to the workingman having his glass of beer, for he liked a glass himself—(laughter)—but he did not worship the cow that gave him milk, neither did he worship the brewer to whom he paid too much for his beer. (Laughter.) Mr. Maginn complained of the state of the approaches to two licensed houses in the district, which he saw made it almost impossible to get into them dry- shod, and asked whether it was through poverty or meanness that those places were left unpaved ? It would be a great boon to the inhabitants of Sankey Bridges if they could have a waiting-room in connection with the tramways. Would it not be better to achieve improvements like those than to go on the platform and preach babble to workingmen? (Applause.) Were not these more homely truths that were brought home to them by John Maginn' the criminal—(langhter)—than were brought home to them the previous night by the array of swells ? (Applause.) He believed that no house should be allowed to be tenanted more than six months before the streets was paved. From the day he entered the Council as the representative of St. John's Ward to the day he was forced to leave it through the power of the same crowd that took the platform the previous night, he was never a party to any " arrangements," but the ratepayers had the best and the worst of him publicly. He promised to act in a similar way if, as he hoped, he was returned for St. Austin's Ward. (Applause.) Questions were then invited, but none were asked.

===== 0002601_19080118_art0111.txt | included=true | included =====
VOTES OF CONFIDENCE.

Mr. S. WRIGHT, in moving a vote of confidence in Mr. Magian, pointed out that Mr. Broadhurst belonged to the party who opposed the passing of the Trades Unions Act which gave the workingmen of this country the facility which they now enjoyed of protecting their funds for their own welfare, Mr. Maginn would act conscientiously for the benefit of the whole community, independent of any clique. (Applause.) Councillor TooLE, in seconding, said Mr.Maginn was coming out under the banner of the party of which he (Councillor Toole) was a member—the independent party. As long as municipal candidates fought under that banner, he would feel it his duty to support them. He hoped Mr. Maginn would be returned, because their party at present was small. It consisted of three 1 members—Councillor Craik, Alderman Evans and him- self, but with the ability and talents of men like Mr. Maginn they would begin to make their presence felt, and perhaps before long they would be as strong as the other parties in the Council. Mr. Maginn was one of the most honest and straightforward men who ever sat in the Council. Because he would not be pulled by any party, he was opposed, and defeated. He trusted that the electors of St. Austin's would show their good sense by returning him by a large majority. (Applause). Mr. J. ELWELL supported, and said the piincipal question was not whether the candidate was a working- man, but who were behind him. There was an old say- ing that those who paid the piper had the right to call the tune. If that were so in the present instance, could Mr. Broadhurst represent them in the Council Chamber as a workingman pure and simple? (Cries of "No " and " Yes.")—Mr. Elwell then showed how the Tory party had acted contrary to the interests of the workers, and appealed to the voters to support Mr. Maginn. The resolution was carried with four dissentients. Mr. MAGINN said he had no donbt there were more than four opponents present, but the others were Con- servatives, and naturally shy. (Laughter.) If they wanted a man out of their own workers at any time to represent them fearlessly, he would advise them to bring forward Mr. Elwell. (Applause.)

MEETING TO-NIGHT.

This (Saturday) evening another meeting in support of Mr. Maginn's candidature will be held in Sankey Bridges Wesleyan schools.

MR. BROADHURST'S CANDIDATURE.

A meeting in support of the candidature of Mu James Broadhurst was held in St. Luke's school o Wednesday evening, when there was a large attendance. Alderman W. J. Forshaw presided and was supported on the platform by Sir Gilbert Greenall and Messrs. R. Pierpoint, W. P. Rylands, J. J. Hands, E. J. Hall, H. Taylor, Councillors Roberts and Wilson, Rev. E. C. E. Carleton, and others.

A letter was received from Mr. John White,regretting his inability to attend. The CHarremAN said they were in good company. (Applause.) He hoped they had all come to the con- clusion that Mr. Broadhurst was the best man to repre- sent St. Austin's Ward. (Applause.) It was very right and proper that the working man should be represented both in the House of Parliament and on the municipal bench by a workingman, and so long as they had a workingman to represent them they would be sure of their interests being served. He hoped they would do the same in St. Austin's Ward as they had done in the Town Hall and Howley Wards. (Applause.) Their candidate, Mr. Broadhurst, was a good and industrious man. He hoped they would return him to the Council on Monday next by a large and substantial majority. _ Mr. BROADHURST thanked them for nominating him as a candidate and said, with great pride, that his pro- posers' seconders and assentors numbered fifty, "In the past," continued the candidate " it has been said that the leaders of the Conservative Party are opposed to the _interest of labour."

Mr. RAND-LTISTEIear, hear. Mr. BROADHURST : I thank you for your hear, hear, Mr. Randles. A VOICP • Out with him. , .

- - . The CANDIDATE: " out with him." Continuing Mr. Broadhurst said the Conservative party were not opposed to the interests of labour but did all in their power to serve the interests of the workingmen. A VOICE : This is not a political meeting. Mr. BROADHURST : I want fair play. (Applause.) When the disturbance , had subsided the candidate said" I thank you from the bottom of my heart. lam not opposed to you Mr. Handles." Continuing Mr. Broadhurst said that he had been a trades unionist since he was twenty-one years of age. He was their representative on the Infirmary Committee and other worthy institutions including the Life-boat Saturday fund. He was their delegate on the Right to Work Committee. In reference to the excessive charges for admittance to the Municipal Baths he thought as they had to work amongst the dirt and filth the charges for admittance to the baths were much too high. The price of the gas and water was rather too high and the profits ought to go to decrease of the rates. (Hear, hear.) He had been asked to give his opinion on the Education Question. As a workingman he was going to give his individual opinion. If a Wesleyan or any- body else wanted to have his child educated in his own faith he had a perfect right to have his citild so educated. He was going to ask them to choose between Mr. Magill!' and himself, and if they thought he (Mr. Broadhurst) was not of the same political faith as they themselves they must weigh Mr. Magiun and himself up. Mr. Maginn called himself an Independent but he was backed up by the Liberal Party. If Mr. Maginn was a successful man in the Council why did they not return him again ? Because he was a complete failure. (Applause.) Mr. ROBERT PIERPOINT then moved a resolution in support of Mr. Broadhurst's candidature. He said he was sorry to say that he was the oldest resident in St. Austin's Ward. He had lived there for thirty years. (Applause.) With regard to the Educa- tion Question he believed that every man should be allowed to have his child educated in any way he pleased. He hoped they would return Mr. Broadhurst at the head of the poll on Monday next. Mr. T. CAMPBELL seconded. Sir GILBERT GREENALL supported, together with Mr. W. P. RYI,INDS, who dealt at length with the Government's Colonial Policy and briefly with the Home policy. The Rev. E. C. E. CARLTON also supported and the resolution was carried unanimously. Mr. RANDLES asked : Is Mr. Broadhurst in favour of Municipal Coal Surfply ? The meeting then broke up, no notice being taken of the question. A meeting in support of Mr. Broadhurst's candidature was held in the Sankey Wesleyan Schools last (Friday) evening, when a resolution was passed similar to the one passed at the previous meeting.
